L18@P1: CONSULAR AFFAIRS
L19@P1: State May Be Unable to Cover Projected Costs if
L20@P1: Revenues Do Not Quickly Rebound to Pre-Pandemic
L21@P1: Levels
L22@P1: What GAO Found
L23@P1: The Department of State provides passport, visa, and overseas citizens services
L24@P1: to millions of Americans and foreign nationals annually and collects user fees for
L25@P1: some of these services. The COVID-19 pandemic discouraged potential travelers
L26@P1: who would pay fees, resulting in a 41 percent decline in consular fee revenue in
L27@P1: fiscal year 2020. To compensate for this decrease in revenue, State used
L28@P1: supplemental and annual appropriations and temporarily expanded expenditure
L29@P1: and transfer authorities provided by several acts of Congress. It also drew down
L30@P1: unobligated balances that it had carried over into fiscal year 2020.
L31@P1: GAO modeled State’s future consular fee revenue stream and costs through
L32@P1: fiscal year 2026 across five scenarios. These scenarios used pessimistic,
L33@P1: neutral, and optimistic revenue outlook projections based on historical consular
L34@P1: fee revenue from fiscal years 2013 through 2020. The modeling indicates that
L35@P1: State’s carryover balances will decline and likely will not meet the targeted
L36@P1: threshold for the consular fee carryover balance in some fiscal years if fee
L37@P1: revenue does not return to pre-pandemic levels in fiscal year 2022 and beyond.
L38@P1: See figure.
L39@P1: Consular and Border Security Program (CBSP) Account Carryover Balance Median
L40@P1: Values across Simulation Results, Fiscal Years 2022–2026
L41@P1: View GAO-22-104424. For more information,
L42@P1: contact Jason Bair at (202) 512-6881 or
L43@P1: bairj@gao.gov.
L44@P1: Why GAO Did This Study
L45@P1: From fiscal years 2013 through 2019,
L46@P1: consular fees fully funded consular
L47@P1: operations, according to State
L48@P1: documentation. However, the COVID19 pandemic caused State’s revenues
L49@P1: from passport and visa fees to drop.
L50@P1: GAO was asked to review consular
L51@P1: fees. This report examines, among
L52@P1: other things, how State managed the
L53@P1: decline in consular fee revenues,
L54@P1: projections regarding State’s ability to
L55@P1: meet the targeted threshold in the
L56@P1: future, and the extent to which State’s
L57@P1: processes for estimating key data meet
L58@P1: the key elements of economic analysis.
L59@P1: GAO reviewed State documentation
L60@P1: and data, modeled projections for the
L61@P1: consular fees account, and interviewed
L62@P1: State officials.
L63@P1: What GAO Recommends
L64@P1: GAO recommends that State (1)
L65@P1: assess what actions would allow it to
L66@P1: cover future consular costs; (2)
L67@P1: measure the statistical variability of unit
L68@P1: costs; and (3) document its cost,
L69@P1: demand, and revenue estimates. State
L70@P1: did not concur with the first
L71@P1: recommendation, indicating it believes
L72@P1: the cost model output is sufficient for
L73@P1: this purpose. State partially concurred
L74@P1: with the other two recommendations,
L75@P1: noting it uses a deterministic math
L76@P1: model to calculate unit costs and it
L77@P1: documents the process for cost
L78@P1: estimates. GAO maintains that a plan
L79@P1: to assess actions that could cover
L80@P1: future costs would better position State
L81@P1: to request statutory changes that align
L82@P1: with actual needs. GAO also maintains
L83@P1: that State should reflect statistical
L84@P1: variability in unit costs because not
L85@P1: doing so many limit its ability to
L86@P1: respond to risks arising from
L87@P1-2: fluctuations in cost. GAO maintains United States Government Accountability Office
L88@P2: Notes: According to State officials, State’s official target is to maintain a minimum 25 percent
L89@P2: carryover balance by retained fee each year. GAO used State’s target to calculate 25 percent of
L90@P2: average historical obligations against the CBSP account as a whole. GAO presents this as the
L91@P2: benchmark value of $803 million and uses this calculation to determine the likelihood of State
L92@P2: meeting the targeted threshold. GAO’s model reflects retained fee amounts as of November 2021.
L93@P2: Accessible Data Table for Highlight Figure
L94@P2: Dollars in millions
L95@P2: FY 2022 FY 2023 FY 2024 FY 2025 FY 2026
L96@P2: Scenario 1 625.37 332.11 267.96 100.53 -180.26
L97@P2: Scenario 2 626.68 663.54 599.08 427.11 151.42
L98@P2: Scenario 3 971.04 1,008.29 943.40 774.95 496.44
L99@P2: Scenario 4 626.19 898.64 836.66 664.45 384.28
L100@P2: Scenario 5 1,214.61 1,252.34 1,185.64 1,016.81 740.49
L101@P2: Additionally, GAO’s simulation also shows that projected revenue may not be
L102@P2: sufficient to cover costs through fiscal year 2026 across all five scenarios. For
L103@P2: example, if State’s consular fee revenue recovers to pre-pandemic levels in fiscal
L104@P2: year 2023 (scenario 2), GAO estimates that the carryover balance is unlikely to
L105@P2: meet the targeted threshold each year through fiscal year 2026. Specifically,
L106@P2: GAO estimates that in this scenario the carryover balance would be $151 million
L107@P2: in fiscal year 2026—hundreds of millions of dollars below the targeted threshold.
L108@P2: According to State, falling below the targeted threshold could put State at risk of
L109@P2: being unable to make necessary obligations at the beginning of the fiscal year.
L110@P2: Consular fee revenue is not received evenly over the course of a year, according
L111@P2: to State officials, instead peaking in spring and summer.
L112@P2: State has requested statutory changes to its authorities to set and use some
L113@P2: consular fees so that it can generate additional revenue or have more flexibility in
L114@P2: how it can use revenue. However, State has not documented its analysis to
L115@P2: support these requests. Without a plan for assessing the potential impact of the
L116@P2: requested changes, State risks recommending statutory changes that do not
L117@P2: align with actual needs. As a result, State could collect consular fees in excess of
L118@P2: its costs for some services, thereby over-charging visa or passport applicants.
L119@P2: State uses estimates of unit costs, demand and revenue to achieve full cost
L120@P2: recovery and to project the sufficiency of consular revenue. State’s process for
L121@P2: estimating unit costs of consular services fully meets two and partially meets
L122@P2: three key elements of economic analysis. Specifically, State does not calculate
L123@P2: the range of potential costs, such as those that result from variations in how
L124@P2: much time it takes to process a visa; as such, State does not calculate the
L125@P2: statistical variability of the resulting unit costs. This lack of transparency on the
L126@P2: plausible range of unit costs may limit management’s ability to identify, analyze,
L127@P2: and respond to risks to full cost recovery arising from fluctuations in obligations.
L128@P2: In addition, State lacks documentation of its processes for estimating demand
L129@P2: and revenue. By not documenting these processes or key analytical decisions,
L130@P2: State risks being unable to retain and share its organizational knowledge on fee
L131@P2: setting, including communicating its compliance with best practices.
L132@P2: What GAO Recommends
L133@P2: that documenting processes for
L134@P2: revenue and demand estimates and
L135@P2: fully documenting processes for cost
L136@P2: estimates would better position State to
L137@P2: identify and respond to risks to full cost
L138@P2: recovery.

L341@P9: Letter
L342@P9: April 18, 2022
L343@P9: The Honorable Gregory W. Meeks
L344@P9: Chairman
L345@P9: The Honorable Michael T. McCaul
L346@P9: Ranking Member
L347@P9: Committee on Foreign Affairs
L348@P9: House of Representatives
L349@P9: The Department of State’s Bureau of Consular Affairs (CA) collects
L350@P9: billions of dollars in consular fee revenue annually—revenue from fees
L351@P9: charged to users for consular services such as passport and visa
L352@P9: issuances, as well as overseas citizen services.1 In 2013, State
L353@P9: implemented its Consular Realignment Initiative and began using
L354@P9: consular fee revenue exclusively to cover consular operations costs.2 This
L355@P9: fee revenue generally is deposited into CA’s Consular and Border
L356@P9: Security Programs (CBSP) account, but State is required to transfer
L357@P9: certain consular fee collections to the General Fund of the U.S.
L358@P9: Government (General Fund), which is managed by the Department of the
L359@P9: Treasury (Treasury).3
L360@P9: In fiscal year 2019, CA collected nearly $4 billion in consular fees. State
L361@P9: officials have identified a structural imbalance in its fee authorities which,
L362@P9: according to these officials, presents a challenge in continuing to fund
L363@P9: consular services with fee revenues. Additionally, the COVID-19
L364@P9: pandemic had a negative impact on travel and the collection of consular
L365@P9: fees in fiscal year 2020, resulting in a decline in fee revenue.
L366@P9: 
L367@P9: 1In this report, we refer to the total amount of money that CA collects from fees charged to
L368@P9: users for consular services as “consular fee collections.” We refer to the amount of that
L369@P9: money that CA can retain and use to cover the costs of providing consular services as
L370@P9: “consular fee revenue.” Some consular fees are statutorily defined as fees and some as
L371@P9: surcharges; however, for the purposes of this report, we refer to them collectively as
L372@P9: “consular fees.”
L373@P9: 2Prior to fiscal year 2013, State relied on a mix of annual appropriations and fee revenues,
L374@P9: according to State officials.
L375@P9: 3State is required by some statutes to deposit certain consular fee collections into the
L376@P9: General Fund. In this report, we refer to the consular fees collected but not retained by
L377@P9-10: State as “transfers to the General Fund.”Letter
L378@P10: Page 2 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L379@P10: You asked us to review State’s consular services fee structure. This
L380@P10: report examines how State manages its consular fee revenue and its
L381@P10: ability to adapt to the economic challenges of the pandemic, including (1)
L382@P10: how consular fee revenues compared to obligations prior to the
L383@P10: pandemic, (2) how State managed the decline in consular fee revenues
L384@P10: as a result of decreased demand for consular services caused by the
L385@P10: pandemic, (3) projections regarding CA’s ability to meet the targeted
L386@P10: thresholds for the CBSP account in the future, and (4) the extent to which
L387@P10: State’s processes for estimating key data for evaluating the adequacy of
L388@P10: its revenue meet the key elements of economic analysis.
L389@P10: To determine how consular fee revenues and obligations compared prior
L390@P10: to the pandemic and how State addressed the decline in consular fee
L391@P10: revenue due to the pandemic, we analyzed aggregated fee revenue,
L392@P10: obligations, and carryover balance data provided to us by State in the
L393@P10: form of multiple summary data tables from several internal agency
L394@P10: databases, including the Global Financial Management System (GFMS),
L395@P10: State’s official financial system of record.4 We analyzed these data from
L396@P10: fiscal years 2013 through 2020, the most recent data available at the time
L397@P10: of our analysis. We analyzed aggregate consular fee revenue,
L398@P10: obligations, and carryover balance data for fiscal years 2020 and 2021, a
L399@P10: period that included the onset of the pandemic. We also analyzed data
L400@P10: from budget justification documents related to supplemental and annual
L401@P10: appropriations provided to State during the pandemic. Finally, we
L402@P10: interviewed knowledgeable State officials to determine how consular fee
L403@P10: revenues changed during the pandemic and how CA adapted to the
L404@P10: challenges and managed its consular fee revenue accordingly.
L405@P10: To examine projections regarding the sufficiency of consular fee revenue
L406@P10: in the future, we developed a model to assess State’s ability to maintain
L407@P10: its CBSP account revenue in order to fund consular services across five
L408@P10: 
L409@P10: 4In this report, we refer to unobligated funding from State’s retained fees in the CBSP
L410@P10: account or other accounts remaining across fiscal years as “carryover balances.” An
L411@P10: obligation is a definite commitment that creates a legal liability of the government for the
L412@P10: payment of goods and services ordered or received, or a legal duty on the part of the
L413@P10: United States that could mature into a legal liability by virtue of actions on the part of the
L414@P10: other party beyond the control of the United States. Payment may be made immediately or
L415@P10: in the future. An agency incurs an obligation, for example, when it places an order, signs a
L416@P10: contract, awards a grant, purchases a service, or takes other actions that require the
L417@P10-11: government to make payments to the public or from one government account to another.Letter
L418@P11: Page 3 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L419@P11: different revenue scenarios through fiscal year 2026.5 We set a range of
L420@P11: three possible future revenue outlooks, defined as (1) pessimistic, which
L421@P11: projects future revenue ranging between the lowest revenue in the
L422@P11: historical data and a standard deviation below the average of historical
L423@P11: revenues; (2) neutral, which projects future revenue ranging within one
L424@P11: standard deviation below and above the average of historical revenues;
L425@P11: or (3) optimistic, which projects future revenue ranging between a
L426@P11: standard deviation above the average of historical revenues and the
L427@P11: highest revenue.6
L428@P11: To assess the reliability of the aggregated revenue, obligations, and
L429@P11: carryover balance data from GFMS and other sources, we reviewed
L430@P11: relevant documentation, interviewed knowledgeable State officials, and
L431@P11: conducted electronic data testing. We determined that these data were
L432@P11: sufficiently reliable for our purposes of analyzing consular fee revenue,
L433@P11: obligations, and carryover balance trends from fiscal years 2013 through
L434@P11: 2021. To assess the reliability of the supplemental and annual
L435@P11: appropriations data, we reviewed the data for accuracy and compared the
L436@P11: values to amounts in official State documents and budget justification
L437@P11: documents. We determined that these data were sufficiently reliable and
L438@P11: accurately documented State’s receipt and management of supplemental
L439@P11: and annual appropriations provided following the onset of the pandemic.
L440@P11: To address the extent to which State’s cost estimation processes meets
L441@P11: key elements of economic analysis, we assessed the strengths and
L442@P11: limitations of State’s processes for estimating the unit costs, demand, and
L443@P11: total revenue of consular services and products against the five key
L444@P11: elements of economic analyses identified in GAO’s Assessment
L445@P11: Methodology for Economic Analysis.
L446@P11: 7 We also compared State’s use of
L447@P11: quality information to achieve its objectives, its analysis and response to
L448@P11: risk as it relates to achieving those objectives, and its documentation of
L449@P11: processes and decisions against GAO’s Standards for Internal Control in
L450@P11:

L452@P11: assessed State’s likelihood of keeping balances for the CBSP account and its fee-specific
L453@P11: subaccounts across the five scenarios.

L463@P12: We conducted this performance audit from August 2020 to April 2022 in
L464@P12: accordance with generally accepted government auditing standards.
L465@P12: Those standards require that we plan and perform the audit to obtain
L466@P12: sufficient, appropriate evidence to provide a reasonable basis for our
L467@P12: findings and conclusions based on our audit objectives. We believe that
L468@P12: the evidence obtained provides a reasonable basis for our findings and
L469@P12: conclusions based on our audit objectives.
L470@P12: Background
L471@P12: CA Provides a Variety of Consular Services
L472@P12: State’s consular services span three areas: (1) passport services, (2) visa
L473@P12: services, and (3) overseas citizen services. Specifically:
L474@P12: · Passport services: CA provides passport services to permit citizens
L475@P12: to travel or temporarily reside in foreign territories and also allows
L476@P12: them access to U.S. consular services and assistance while abroad.
L477@P12: CA charges several fees for passports, including the passport security
L478@P12: surcharge (PSS) and the Western Hemisphere Travel Initiative
L479@P12: (WHTI) surcharge.
L480@P12: · Visa services: CA provides services to foreign nationals applying for
L481@P12: immigrant and nonimmigrant visas at U.S. embassies and consulates
L482@P12: in other countries. CA charges several fees for immigrant and
L483@P12: nonimmigrant visas, including the machine readable visa (MRV) fee
L484@P12: and the H and L fraud prevention and detection fee.
L485@P12: · Overseas citizen services: CA provides emergency and nonemergency services to help U.S. citizens abroad, such as visiting
L486@P12: them if they are imprisoned, assisting next-of-kin after the death of a
L487@P12: U.S. citizen abroad, and issuing consular reports of births abroad as
L488@P12: an official record of a child’s claim to U.S. citizenship or nationality.
L489@P12: Figure 1 shows examples of consular services that State provides.
L490@P12: 
L491@P12: 8GAO, Standards for Internal Control in the Federal Government, GAO-14-704G
L492@P12-13: (Washington, D.C.: Sept. 10, 2014). Letter
L493@P13: Page 5 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L494@P13: Figure 1: Examples of Department of State Consular Services
L495@P13: CA Does Not Charge a Fee for Some Consular Services
L496@P13: CA does not charge a fee for some consular services because those
L497@P13: services are to be provided free of charge pursuant to statute, or because
L498@P13: State has made a policy or regulatory decision not to charge a fee for
L499@P13: these services. Examples include some death and estate services, some
L500@P13: visas applications as part of U.S. government-sponsored exchanges, visa
L501@P13: applications for diplomats and employees of international organizations,
L502@P13: and special immigrant visa applications from Iraq and Afghanistan under
L503@P13: a program for individuals who worked with the United States in those
L504@P13: countries.
L505@P13: Historically, CA covered the cost of certain consular services provided at
L506@P13: no cost to users and services for which State does not retain a fee by
L507@P13: using MRV fee revenues, according to State officials.9 However, State
L508@P13: does not have the authority to set the MRV fee higher than the cost of
L509@P13: that particular service to cover the costs of other services for which it
L510@P13: does not charge a fee, according to State documentation.
L511@P13: 
L512@P13: 9The full list of the fees that CA charges for consular services are listed in 22 C.F.R. §
L513@P13-14: 22.1, Schedule of Fees for Consular Services-Department of State and Foreign Service.Letter
L514@P14: Page 6 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L515@P14: The Price for a Particular Service Can Reflect Multiple
L516@P14: Fees
L517@P14: The total amount an individual pays for a consular service may reflect
L518@P14: several fees combined into one charged amount. For example, as figure 2
L519@P14: shows, the application price of $130 for an adult passport book includes
L520@P14: three fees: the $80 PSS, the $22 WHTI surcharge, and a $28 passport
L521@P14: application fee.10
L522@P14: Figure 2: Department of State Consular Fees for an Adult Passport Book
L523@P14: Note: The General Fund of the U.S. Government is managed by the Department of the Treasury.
L524@P14: State is required by law to deposit certain consular fee collections into the General Fund, which we
L525@P14: refer to as “transfers to the General Fund.” The $130 application price for an adult passport book
L526@P14: does not include a $35 passport execution fee required for first-time applicants and others who must
L527@P14: apply in person. Passport execution fees charged by acceptance facility partners (e.g., libraries, post
L528@P14: offices, and courthouses) are retained by those partners, and any other passport execution fees paid
L529@P14: to State officials, domestically or abroad, are transferred to the General Fund, according to State
L530@P14: officials. The Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2022, Pub. L. No. 117-103, § 7069(e), enacted on
L531@P14:

L549@P16: CA Charges Fees for Consular Services under Several
L550@P16: Different Legal Authorities
L551@P16: State Has Multiple Fee Authorities That Allow It to Retain Consular
L552@P16: Fee Revenues for Varying Purposes
L553@P16: CA retains revenues under multiple consular fee authorities. All of the
L554@P16: fees for which revenue is retained are charged for visa or passport
L555@P16: services. These fees were established by different statutes and at
L556@P16: different times, and all were established before CA transitioned to fully
L557@P16: funding consular operations with fee revenue in fiscal year 2013.11
L558@P16: Specifically:
L559@P16: · MRV fee: This fee is charged for nonimmigrant visas and combined
L560@P16: adult border crossing card and nonimmigrant visa applications. The
L561@P16: fee ranges from $160 to $265 for non-petition- and petition-based
L562@P16: visas for which State retains fee revenue.12 The MRV fee charged
L563@P16: specifically for border crossing cards is $160 for applicants age 15
L564@P16: and over, and $15 for applicants under age 15.
L565@P16: · PSS: This fee is charged for passport services in support of enhanced
L566@P16: border security. The fee is $80 for a passport book. There is no PSS
L567@P16: fee associated with a passport card.
L568@P16: · WHTI surcharge: This fee is charged for passport services. It was
L569@P16: introduced to cover the costs of meeting an increased demand for
L570@P16: passports that resulted from actions taken to comply with a provision
L571@P16: in the Intelligence Reform and Terrorism Prevention Act of 2004.13
L572@P16: 
L573@P16: 11Additionally, a new authority included in the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2022,
L574@P16: Pub. L. No. 117-103, § 7069(e) (March 15, 2022), requires that passport application and
L575@P16: execution fees be deposited in the CBSP account. This report does not reflect this new
L576@P16: authority. We refer to the fee authorities that State had as of February 2022 as “the nine
L577@P16: retained fees” in this report.
L578@P16: 12The MRV fee is $160 for non-petition-based visas (except E-category); $205 for nonpetition-based E-category visas; $190 for petition-based H, L, O, P, Q, and R visas; and
L579@P16: $265 for petition-based K-visas.
L580@P16: 13Pub. L. No. 108-459, § 7209, 118 Stat. 3823 (Dec. 17, 2004) (codified as amended at 8
L581@P16-17: U.S.C. § 1185 note). Letter

L583@P17: The fee is $22 for an adult passport book or card, and $44 for an adult
L584@P17: passport book and card, according to State documentation.14
L585@P17: · Expedited passport fee: This fee is charged for the expedited
L586@P17: processing of passports. The fee is $60.
L587@P17: · Immigrant visa security surcharge (IVSS): This fee is charged for
L588@P17: immigrant visas in support of enhanced border security. The fee,
L589@P17: which is embedded in the Immigrant Visa Application Processing fee,
L590@P17: is $100, according to State documentation.
L591@P17: · H and L fraud prevention and detection fee: This fee is charged by
L592@P17: either State or U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services (USCIS) to
L593@P17: cover activities related to preventing and detecting fraud in petitionbased non-immigrant work visas, according to State documentation.15
L594@P17: The fee may be $150 or $500, according to State officials.16
L595@P17: · Affidavit of support fee: This fee is charged for visa services given
L596@P17: to a sponsor who provides an affidavit of support under a provision in
L597@P17: the Immigration and Nationality Act of 1952.17 The fee is $120 and
L598@P17: only applies when reviewed domestically.
L599@P17: · Diversity visa fee: This fee is charged for allocating and processing
L600@P17: applications for visas of applicants selected from the diversity visa
L601@P17: lottery program.18 This fee is $330.
L602@P17: · J-waiver fee: This fee is charged as a processing fee for waiver of
L603@P17: the two-year residency requirement for J-1 visa holders who wish to
L604@P17: 14The WHTI surcharge is $20 for a minor passport book, $15 for a minor passport card,
L605@P17: and $35 for a minor passport book and card, according to State documentation.

L694@P19: aState officials characterize a consular fee as having “broad” expenditure authority if the revenues
L695@P19: from the fee can be used to cover the costs of any consular service, not just a specific type of service
L696@P19: or cost, and are not limited to the type of service for which the fee was charged. State officials
L697@P19: characterize a consular fee as having “narrow” expenditure authority if the revenues from the fee can
L698@P19: only cover the costs of the consular service for which the fee was charged or are otherwise limited to
L699@P19: a specific type of service or cost.
L700@P19: bFor the purposes of using PSS fee revenues, enhanced border-security costs include the costs of
L701@P19: secure book and card materials; passport printers; and compensation associated with domestic
L702@P19: passport adjudication, including fraud prevention, according to State documentation.
L703@P19: cWhile State’s fee authority permits expedited passport fee revenues to be used to cover the costs of
L704@P19: providing consular services, State chose not to make these funds available to specifically cover the
L705@P19: costs of consular services until fiscal year 2020, the first year in a 3-year plan, according to State
L706@P19: documentation and officials. This plan transitioned expedited passport fee revenues from supporting
L707@P19: the Information Technology Central Fund to supporting the Consular and Border Security Programs
L708@P19: (CBSP) account, with gradually increasing amounts allocated to the CBSP. State planned to allocate
L709@P19: approximately 27 percent of expedited passport fee revenues to the CBSP in fiscal year 2020, 66
L710@P19: percent in fiscal year 2021, and 100 percent in fiscal year 2022. However, in fiscal year 2020,
L711@P19: because of decreased demand, the CBSP received $15 million of the $131 million total revenue (11
L712@P19: percent) collected in expedited passport fees, with the remaining balance used to support the
L713@P19: Information Technology Central Fund.
L714@P19: dFor the purposes of using IVSS fee revenues, enhanced border-security costs include the costs of
L715@P19: enhanced security screening requirements associated with fingerprint collection and the time spent by
L716@P19: consular officers related to enhanced border security in processing immigrant visa applications,
L717@P19-20: according to State documentation.Letter

L723@P20: CA Transfers All Other Collections to the General Fund
L724@P20: CA does not retain collections from fees other than those described
L725@P20: above.23 Instead, State is required by law to transfer these collections to
L726@P20: the General Fund.24 For example, CA does not retain any fees collected
L727@P20: for overseas citizen services and transfers certain visa fees to the
L728@P20: General Fund. Most of the fees that CA does not retain are transferred to
L729@P20: the General Fund on a real-time, rolling basis, according to State
L730@P20: officials.25
L731@P20: State Deposits Most Retained Consular Fees in the
L732@P20: Consular and Border Security Programs Account
L733@P20: Created in 2017 by legislation, according to State officials, the CBSP was
L734@P20: established at the beginning of fiscal year 2019 as a stand-alone account
L735@P20: into which revenues from various existing fee authorities were to be
L736@P20: deposited for the purposes of consular and border security programs.
L737@P20: This account was established independent of the larger Diplomatic and
L738@P20: Consular Programs account, now known as the Diplomatic Programs
L739@P20:

L741@P20: Appropriations Act, 2022, Pub. L. No. 117-103, § 7069(e) (March 15, 2022), which
L742@P20: provides that, beginning on October 1, 2021 and for each fiscal year thereafter, passport
L743@P20: application and execution fees shall be deposited in the CBSP account.

L757@P21: account.26 According to the President’s budget for fiscal year 2019, the

L764@P21: The CBSP account is a single account, strictly reserved for consular and

L766@P21: authorities. CA deposits consular fee collections in the CBSP for six of the

L768@P21: account include the MRV fee, PSS, WHTI surcharge, IVSS, affidavit of

L770@P21: Carryover balances are unobligated funds from State’s retained fees
L771@P21: remaining in the CBSP account or other accounts across fiscal years.29
L772@P21: According to State officials, CA does not use carryover balances any
L773@P21: differently from fee revenue collected in the current fiscal year. All fee
L774@P21: revenue within the CBSP account is “no-year” funding, meaning that it is
L775@P21: available for obligation for an indefinite period of time and available until
L776@P21: expended.

L784@P21: account. CA is setting up a subaccount for the expedited passport fee, which will be

L786@P21: According to statute, State is required to deposit H and L fee revenues into the Fraud
L787@P21: Prevention and Detection account, which is available to the Departments of State,
L788@P21: Homeland Security, and Labor to combat fraud (each receiving one-third of the amounts
L789@P21: deposited). Treasury makes available a portion of the new collections to the CBSP
L790@P21: account monthly, according to State officials. J-waiver fee revenues are deposited into the
L791@P21: Diplomatic Programs account with funds from other exchange and educational programs,
L792@P21: according to State officials.
L793@P21: 29In this report, we refer to unobligated funding from State’s retained fees in the CBSP
L794@P21: account and other accounts remaining across fiscal years as “carryover balances.” State
L795@P21-22: refers to these carryover balances as “carryforward balances.”Letter
L796@P22: Page 14 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L797@P22: State Models Cost and Recommends Changes as Part of
L798@P22: the Consular Fee­Setting Process
L799@P22: CA officials use a cost model to generate estimates of unit cost for
L800@P22: various consular services, which they combine with estimated demand to
L801@P22: inform potential changes to consular fees.30 The amount of some consular
L802@P22: fees is set by statute, while statutory authorities for other fees authorize
L803@P22: State to set the fee amount. For consular services for which CA sets the
L804@P22: fee, it does so based on the concept of full cost recovery, according to
L805@P22: State officials.31
L806@P22: CA sets fee amounts for consular services to cover the cost of processing
L807@P22: applications or providing specific services.32 The MRV fee, for example, is
L808@P22: designed to cover the entire cost of service for processing the
L809@P22: nonimmigrant visa application and is charged whether or not the applicant
L810@P22: receives a visa, according to State’s Foreign Affairs Handbooks (FAH).33
L811@P22: Cost of Service Model
L812@P22: CA uses its Cost of Service Model (CoSM) to calculate unit costs of
L813@P22: individual services based on the total cost to the U.S. government of
L814@P22: providing that consular service. The model uses commercially available,
L815@P22: activity-based cost software. CA’s CoSM team works with internal and
L816@P22: 
L817@P22: 30Projected collections are then estimated based on the same assumed demand that was
L818@P22: used to calculate the unit costs and used as support that the unit costs are sufficient,
L819@P22: according to State officials.
L820@P22: 31State points to provisions in OMB Circular No. A-25 as the basis for this position. This
L821@P22: guidance requires, in general, that agencies set user charges so that they will be sufficient
L822@P22: to recover the full cost to the federal government of providing the service, resource, or
L823@P22: good. Under the guidance, agencies are to include all direct and indirect costs when
L824@P22: determining full cost, including but not limited to personnel costs, including salaries and
L825@P22: benefits such as medical insurance and retirement; physical overhead; consulting;
L826@P22: material and supply costs; utilities; insurance; travel; rents or imputed rents on land,
L827@P22: buildings, and equipment; management and supervisory costs; costs of collecting and
L828@P22: enforcing fees; research; establishment of standards and regulation; and imputed costs,
L829@P22: such as depreciation on structures.
L830@P22: 32While CA may set a fee based on the costs of providing a particular service, in some
L831@P22: cases, it may use the fee revenue it collects from providing the service more broadly than
L832@P22: on just covering the cost of the service. For example, the MRV fee is set based on the
L833@P22: costs CA incurs in processing machine readable nonimmigrant visas and machine
L834@P22: readable combined border crossing identification cards and nonimmigrant visas, but CA
L835@P22: can use MRV fee revenue broadly to cover the costs of providing consular services.
L836@P22-23: 33Department of State, 7 FAH-1 H-710, “Consular Fees.” Letter
L837@P23: Page 15 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L838@P23: external stakeholders to collect data required to calculate both domestic
L839@P23: and international costs of consular services, according to State
L840@P23: documentation and officials.
L841@P23: The model calculates total costs and raw unit costs for consular services.
L842@P23: CA uses the CoSM to recommend appropriate fee amounts for services
L843@P23: and inform CA’s funding strategies so that CA appropriately administers
L844@P23: the CBSP, according to State officials. The methodology has not changed
L845@P23: significantly over time, according to State officials.
L846@P23: Sources of Data for Fee Setting
L847@P23: CA uses cost, workload (volume of or demand for consular services), and
L848@P23: level of effort data from multiple sources as inputs to the CoSM for the
L849@P23: purpose of developing unit costs to inform fee setting. These sources
L850@P23: include but are not limited to:
L851@P23: · GFMS: State’s official accounting system for all department cost data
L852@P23: is the source of the financial (cost) data inputs to the CoSM.
L853@P23: · Consular Consolidated Database: This system provides CA with a
L854@P23: near real-time aggregate of all consular activity data and is used to
L855@P23: track and report volume/demand for both nonimmigrant and immigrant
L856@P23: visas, according to State documentation.34
L857@P23: · Travel Documents Issuance System: This system of record for
L858@P23: State’s Passport Office is used to track and report domestic passport
L859@P23: volumes.
L860@P23: · Consular Overseas Data Collection (CODaC): The CODaC is a
L861@P23: survey tool administered by the CA Comptroller’s Office to calculate a
L862@P23: global average cycle time required to perform consular services
L863@P23: overseas. It is used to estimate the level of effort needed to provide
L864@P23: consular services overseas (such as the amount of time a consular
L865@P23: officer spends on processing an application) to support inputs into the
L866@P23: CoSM.
L867@P23: 
L868@P23: 34The recently launched Consular Launchpad for Enterprise Analytics and Reporting
L869@P23: portal will provide front-end access to data, reports, and data visualizations; supplement
L870@P23: Consular Consolidated Database capabilities; and replace some Consular Consolidated
L871@P23-24: Database functions related to the CoSM, according to State documentation. Letter
L872@P24: Page 16 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L873@P24: Recommending Fee Changes and Rulemaking Process
L874@P24: State and Office of Management and Budget (OMB) officials review
L875@P24: proposed changes to consular fee amounts before any changes are
L876@P24: submitted to the federal rulemaking process, which typically takes 1 to 2
L877@P24: years to finalize, according to State documentation and officials. State
L878@P24: typically proposes fee adjustments every other year because it is a multiyear process, according to State documentation and officials. Decisions
L879@P24: about fee adjustments are set at a rate that is projected to cover the costs
L880@P24: of the budget requirements 2 years in advance.35
L881@P24: After CA officials update the CoSM and calculate unit costs, the feesetting process includes:
L882@P24: · State review and fee recommendation decisions: CA officials brief
L883@P24: internal State stakeholders on the calculated unit costs, including
L884@P24: explanations of any changes from previous model results, and review
L885@P24: assumptions about demand. State’s Under Secretary for Management
L886@P24: ultimately determines proposed fee adjustments based on the final fee
L887@P24: recommendations made by interdepartmental leadership.
L888@P24: · OMB review: State officials then brief OMB on proposed fee
L889@P24: changes, and OMB officials concur before any potential fee changes
L890@P24: advance to the rulemaking process.
L891@P24: · Rulemaking process: State and OMB officials work to draft a
L892@P24: proposed rule based on the approved fee recommendations. The
L893@P24: proposed rule is reviewed and approved by State and OMB officials,
L894@P24: then published in the Federal Register for public comment. A final rule
L895@P24: is then drafted to address public comments, reviewed by State and
L896@P24: OMB, and published in the Federal Register. The fee changes are
L897@P24: then implemented, including updates to the Schedule of Fees.36
L898@P24: 
L899@P24: 35As an example of future costs considered as part of proposed fee changes, State
L900@P24: officials noted that they consider the cost assumptions for next-generation passports
L901@P24: (including the purchase of expensive new printers) as part of the fee-setting process.
L902@P24: State’s CoSM team manually adjusts the model to account for these new, anticipated
L903@P24: costs since they are included in the CBSP budget, according to State officials.
L904@P24-25: 3622 C.F.R. § 22.1.Letter
L905@P25: Page 17 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L906@P25: Prior to the Pandemic, Consular Fee Revenue
L907@P25: Exceeded Obligations, but Some Funds Were
L908@P25: Available for Limited Purposes
L909@P25: Prior to the COVID-19 pandemic, CA collected more in consular fee
L910@P25: revenue than it used to fund consular services each year from fiscal years
L911@P25: 2013 through 2019.37 During this period, consular fee revenue from visa
L912@P25: and passport services fully funded consular operations, according to
L913@P25: State documentation, and CA used revenue from the MRV fee to cover
L914@P25: nearly two-thirds of obligations on average for consular services. CA’s
L915@P25: total CBSP account balance grew in most of these fiscal years, and
L916@P25: ended fiscal year 2019 with about $2 billion in carryover balances. CA
L917@P25: accumulated higher carryover balances of fee revenues in CBSP feespecific subaccounts38 with more limited expenditure authorities. For
L918@P25: example, the PSS could only be used to cover the costs of enhanced
L919@P25: border security-related activities, as opposed to the costs of providing
L920@P25: consular services broadly, according to State officials.
L921@P25: Consular Fee Revenues Exceeded Costs of Consular
L922@P25: Services from Fiscal Years 2013 through 2019
L923@P25: CA collected more in consular fee revenues than it used to cover the
L924@P25: costs of consular services each year from fiscal years 2013 through 2019,
L925@P25: spanning the first year that consular services were fully fee-funded (fiscal
L926@P25: year 2013, according to State officials) through the last full fiscal year
L927@P25: before the start of the pandemic (fiscal year 2019). On average during
L928@P25: these years, CA retained $3.6 billion in consular fee revenue annually and
L929@P25: obligated $3.2 billion annually to cover the costs of providing consular
L930@P25: services, retaining more in fee revenues than it used. CA covered more
L931@P25: than 60 percent of the costs of consular services on average using MRV
L932@P25: fee revenues during this period.
L933@P25: 
L934@P25: 37Consular fee revenues, as presented in this report, do not include the amount of
L935@P25: consular fee collections transferred to the General Fund. Revenues described in this
L936@P25: report reflect only the consular fees retained by State.
L937@P25: 38In this report, when we refer to “fee-specific subaccounts” we mean accounts
L938@P25: maintained by State within the CBSP account in order to keep track of revenue from the
L939@P25-26: various retained fees that are deposited into this account. Letter

L1097@P33: A reliance on MRV fee revenues to fund a range of consular services,
L1098@P33: while also transferring certain fee collections to the General Fund and not
L1099@P33: being able to charge any fee for some services, has created a structural
L1100@P33: imbalance in the CBSP, according to State officials. From fiscal years
L1101@P33: 2013 through 2019, CA obligated a total of $22.6 billion to provide
L1102@P33: consular services. CA covered 61 percent of these costs ($13.8 billion)
L1103@P33: with MRV fee revenues, on average. During this period, 55 percent of
L1104@P33: consular fee revenue came from the MRV fee, on average. CA relied
L1105@P33: more heavily on MRV fee revenues to fund consular operations because
L1106@P33: it had the ability to use these revenues broadly on any consular service,
L1107@P33: according to State officials.
L1108@P33: CA Accumulated About $2 Billion in Carryover Balance by

L1110@P33: Subaccounts with Narrower Expenditure Authorities
L1111@P33: CA ended fiscal year 2013, the first year consular operations were fully
L1112@P33: fee-funded, according to State officials, with $1.19 billion in total carryover
L1113@P33: balance funding into fiscal year 2014. In each of the next six fiscal years,
L1114@P33: CA collected and retained more in consular fee revenue than it used to
L1115@P33: cover the costs of consular services, resulting in a total carryover balance
L1116@P33: that generally grew over time. By the end of fiscal year 2019, the last full
L1117@P33: fiscal year prior to the start of the pandemic, CA had accumulated a total
L1118@P33: carryover balance of approximately $2.0 billion, an amount equivalent to
L1119@P33: 54 percent of its obligations on consular services that year. CA considers
L1120@P33:

L1169@P35: aState officials characterize a consular fee as having “broad” expenditure authority if the revenues
L1170@P35: from the fee can be used to cover the costs of any consular service, not just a specific type of service
L1171@P35: or cost, and are not limited to the type of service for which the fee was charged. State officials
L1172@P35: characterize a consular fee as having “narrow” expenditure authority if the revenues from the fee can
L1173@P35: only cover the costs of the consular service for which the fee was charged or are otherwise limited to
L1174@P35: a specific type of service or cost.
L1175@P35: Notes: From fiscal years 2013 through 2019, State retained collections from the expedited passport
L1176@P35: fee, but these revenues were transferred to State’s Information Technology Central Fund and were
L1177@P35: not made available to cover the costs of consular operations through the Consular and Border
L1178@P35: Security Programs account, according to State documentation and officials. State’s carryover balance
L1179@P35: growth each year (fiscal years 2013 through 2019) generally was equivalent to the amount of
L1180@P35: consular fee revenues retained (less expedited passport fee revenues transferred to the Information
L1181@P35: Technology Central Fund) less Consular and Border Security Programs obligations to cover the costs
L1182@P35: of consular services.
L1183@P35: Although its total carryover balance grew from fiscal years 2013 through
L1184@P35: 2019, CA’s carryover balances in individual fee-specific subaccounts
L1185@P35: fluctuated as fee revenues varied by type of service. In addition, as of
L1186@P35: fiscal year 2019, CA generally drew down carryover balances in
L1187@P35: subaccounts with broader expenditure authorities (where State has
L1188@P35: determined that revenues can be used to cover the costs of providing any
L1189@P35: consular service), such as the MRV fee. At the same time, State
L1190@P35: accumulated larger carryover balances in other subaccounts, such as the
L1191@P35: PSS and WHTI fee, with more narrow expenditure authorities. By the end
L1192@P35: of fiscal year 2019, more than two-thirds of the total carryover balance
L1193@P35: was in PSS and WHTI fee revenues, and MRV fee revenues with broader
L1194@P35: expenditure authority accounted for less than one-quarter of the total
L1195@P35: carryover balance.
L1196@P35: CA has relied on MRV fee revenues to cover the costs of consular
L1197@P35: services, according to State officials, although MRV fee revenues
L1198@P35: fluctuated in fiscal years 2013 through 2019. CA’s MRV fee carryover
L1199@P35: balance grew from fiscal years 2013 through 2017. This growth resulted
L1200@P35-36: from general increases in the number of visa applications received and Letter
L1201@P36: Page 28 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L1202@P36: MRV fees paid, according to State documentation and officials. Demand
L1203@P36: for nonimmigrant visa services began to decrease in fiscal year 2017,
L1204@P36: leading to a decrease in MRV fee revenues. As a result, CA began to
L1205@P36: draw down the MRV fee carryover balance in fiscal year 2017 to continue
L1206@P36: covering the costs of overseas citizen services, according to State
L1207@P36: documentation and officials.
L1208@P36: Meanwhile, PSS carryover balances grew in 4 of the 7 fiscal years from
L1209@P36: 2013 through 2019, and WHTI carryover balances grew in 5 of the same
L1210@P36: 7 fiscal years. The volume of passport services provided and associated
L1211@P36: PSS and WHTI fee revenues also generally increased in those 7 fiscal
L1212@P36: years. In fiscal year 2016, CA drew down the PSS and WHTI carryover
L1213@P36: balances to cover costs associated with an anticipated surge in passport
L1214@P36: applications on the tenth anniversary of the WHTI implementation.44 PSS
L1215@P36: and WHTI carryover balances generally grew over time, as shown in
L1216@P36: figure 8. According to State officials, this growth occurred in part because
L1217@P36: CA could only use these revenues on enhanced border security-related
L1218@P36: costs or the costs of meeting increased demand for passports, and
L1219@P36: because CA was not able to use these revenues to cover the cost of
L1220@P36: providing consular services broadly.
L1221@P36: 
L1222@P36: 44The tenth anniversary of the WHTI fell in fiscal year 2017 and generated a surge in
L1223@P36: demand for passport renewals because adult passports have a 10-year validity period.
L1224@P36: Ahead of the anniversary, in fiscal year 2016 CA launched a public relations campaign to
L1225@P36: remind U.S. citizens to renew their passports. CA also purchased needed supplies and
L1226@P36: hired additional contractors and employees to adjudicate the high number of applications,
L1227@P36: according to State documentation. CA used the PSS and WHTI carryover balances to
L1228@P36-37: cover these costs.Letter

L1263@P39: State Used Supplemental and Annual
L1264@P39: Appropriations and Expenditure Flexibilities to
L1265@P39: Address the Decline in Consular Fee Revenue
L1266@P39: Caused by the Pandemic
L1267@P39: State was able to offset the decline in consular fee revenue in fiscal year
L1268@P39: 2020, largely attributed to the COVID-19 pandemic and its adverse effect
L1269@P39: on international travel, by relying on (1) supplemental and annual
L1270@P39: appropriations, (2) temporarily expanded expenditure and transfer
L1271@P39: authorities, and (3) the drawing down of existing carryover balances. In
L1272@P39: this way, CA was able to continue consular operations and offset the
L1273@P39: decrease in fees and surcharges collected and deposited into the CBSP
L1274@P39: account.
L1275@P39: Consular Fee Revenue Declined from $4 Billion in Fiscal
L1276@P39: Year 2019 to $2.3 Billion in Fiscal Year 2020
L1277@P39: The pandemic adversely affected international travel, resulting in a
L1278@P39: decline in passport and visa applications and a corresponding decline in
L1279@P39: the fee revenue collected for these services. Specifically, in fiscal year
L1280@P39: 2019, CA collected nearly $4 billion in consular fee revenue, but in fiscal
L1281@P39: year 2020, the total revenue had decreased by 41 percent to $2.3 billion
L1282@P39: in large part because of the pandemic, according to CA data. Consular
L1283@P39: services provided fell approximately 45 percent in fiscal year 2020, with
L1284@P39: demand for nonimmigrant visas services down nearly 55 percent and
L1285@P39: passport services down more than 40 percent. Consular fee revenue has
L1286@P39: not yet returned to fiscal year 2019 levels, as shown in figure 9. CA also
L1287@P39: processed thousands of special immigrant visas in fiscal year 2021, some
L1288@P39: of which do not require a fee, largely because of a massive influx of
L1289@P39-40: Afghan citizens, according to State documents.Letter

L1443@P45: State Used Carryover Balances to Cover the Costs of Providing
L1444@P45: Consular Services
L1445@P45: Carryover balances from the prior fiscal year were another source of
L1446@P45: revenue used by CA to address the decline in consular fee revenue. CA
L1447@P45: drew down its carryover balances in fiscal years 2020 and 2021 from the
L1448@P45: nine consular fees from which fee revenue is retained. According to State
L1449@P45: data, CA carried over nearly $2 billion from the end of fiscal year 2019
L1450@P45: into 2020. However, because of the pandemic’s adverse effect on
L1451@P45: consular fee revenue, CA carried over $1.2 billion from the end of fiscal
L1452@P45: year 2020 into 2021, a 39 percent decrease from the prior year.
L1453@P45: State’s CBSP Account Carryover Balance
L1454@P45: Unlikely to Meet the Targeted Threshold if
L1455@P45: Revenue Does Not Quickly Rebound, and
L1456@P45: State Has Requested Changes to Address
L1457@P45: Anticipated Revenue Instability
L1458@P45: We developed a model to assess CA’s ability to meet the targeted CBSP
L1459@P45: account carryover balance of 25 percent of obligations across five
L1460@P45: different revenue scenarios through fiscal year 2026. We project that CA’s
L1461@P45: CBSP account carryover balance will decline and is not likely to meet the
L1462@P45: targeted threshold if revenues do not return to pre-pandemic levels in
L1463@P45: fiscal year 2022.57 In addition to assessing the CBSP carryover balance,
L1464@P45: we assessed the likelihood of annual revenues retained in each feespecific subaccount within the CBSP account being sufficient to cover
L1465@P45: projected costs based upon historical obligations against each
L1466@P45: subaccount across the five different revenue scenarios through fiscal year
L1467@P45: 2026. State has requested statutory changes to its fee authorities to
L1468@P45: provide CA greater flexibility in how it sets consular fees or uses consular
L1469@P45: 56The Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2022, § 7069(c) provided this authority through
L1470@P45: fiscal year 2022.
L1471@P45: 57We used CA’s 25 percent carryover target by retained fee each year in our model, by
L1472@P45: calculating 25 percent of average historical obligations against the CBSP account as a
L1473@P45: whole. We present this as the benchmark value and use this calculation to determine the
L1474@P45-46: likelihood of State meeting the targeted threshold.Letter
L1475@P46: Page 38 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L1476@P46: fee revenues in each of the last 8 fiscal years. However, CA has not
L1477@P46: documented its analysis of the potential impact the requested changes
L1478@P46: would have on its carryover balances or its ability to cover the costs of
L1479@P46: consular services.
L1480@P46: Our Model Simulated Five Scenarios Reflecting Various
L1481@P46: Outlooks on Future Revenue
L1482@P46: To model CA’s future revenue stream through fiscal year 2026, we used
L1483@P46: five scenarios that combined pessimistic, neutral, and optimistic revenue
L1484@P46: outlook projections. We classified the revenue projections into these
L1485@P46: categories on the basis of State’s historical revenue information from
L1486@P46: fiscal years 2013 through 2020. Figure 12 shows the construction of the
L1487@P46: projected revenue outlooks used in our projection model.
L1488@P46: Figure 12: Possible Outlooks for Future Department of State Bureau of Consular
L1489@P46: Affairs Fee Revenues
L1490@P46: Notes: Absolute deviation is the average of the absolute value of the difference between the data
L1491@P46: points and their mean. We relied on historical data from State’s Bureau of Consular Affairs from the
L1492@P46: period of fiscal years 2013 through 2020. The ranges associated with the revenue outlooks for the
L1493@P46: CBSP account and its fee-specific subaccounts vary; see GAO-22-104424, appendix V for more
L1494@P46: details.
L1495@P46: The following five scenarios reflect various assumptions about CA’s future
L1496@P46-47: revenue outlooks:Letter
L1497@P47: Page 39 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L1498@P47: · Scenario 1 assumes a slow recovery of future revenues with CA’s
L1499@P47: revenue not returning to pre-COVID-19 pandemic levels until fiscal
L1500@P47: year 2024. Specifically, scenario 1 assumes pessimistic revenue in
L1501@P47: fiscal years 2022 and 2023, then neutral in fiscal year 2024 and
L1502@P47: thereafter.
L1503@P47: · Scenario 2 assumes CA’s revenue returns to pre-pandemic levels in
L1504@P47: fiscal year 2023. Specifically, scenario 2 assumes pessimistic revenue
L1505@P47: in fiscal year 2022, then neutral in fiscal year 2023 and thereafter.
L1506@P47: · Scenario 3 assumes the status quo with CA’s revenue returning to
L1507@P47: pre-pandemic levels in fiscal year 2022. Specifically, scenario 3
L1508@P47: assumes neutral revenue in fiscal year 2022 and thereafter.
L1509@P47: · Scenario 4 assumes an initial slow recovery, followed by a postpandemic surge, and then a return to the status quo. Specifically, with
L1510@P47: this scenario we assume CA’s revenue does not return to prepandemic levels in fiscal year 2022, has a post-pandemic surge in
L1511@P47: fiscal year 2023, and returns to pre-pandemic levels in fiscal year
L1512@P47: 2024. Specifically, scenario 4 assumes pessimistic revenue in fiscal
L1513@P47: year 2022, optimistic in fiscal year 2023, and neutral in fiscal year
L1514@P47: 2024 and thereafter.
L1515@P47: · Scenario 5 assumes CA’s revenue has a post-pandemic surge in
L1516@P47: fiscal year 2022 and returns to pre-pandemic levels in fiscal year
L1517@P47: 2023. Specifically, scenario 5 assumes optimistic revenue in fiscal
L1518@P47: year 2022 and neutral thereafter.
L1519@P47: To assess the likelihood of CA meeting the targeted CBSP account
L1520@P47: carryover balance of 25 percent of obligations and the likelihood of yearly
L1521@P47: revenue retained in each fee-specific subaccount being sufficient to cover
L1522@P47: yearly projected costs based on historic obligations against each
L1523@P47: subaccount across these five scenarios, we also made several
L1524@P47-48: assumptions to control for other future uncertainties (see text box below).Letter
L1525@P48: Page 40 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L1526@P48: GAO Projection Model for the Department of State’s Consular and Border Security Programs (CBSP) Balances:
L1527@P48: Additional Assumptions and Considerations
L1528@P48: We developed a model to assess State’s ability to meet the targeted CBSP account carryover balance and the likelihood that
L1529@P48: annual revenues for fee-specific subaccounts would be sufficient to cover projected costs based upon historical obligations
L1530@P48: across five different revenue scenarios through fiscal year 2026. Our model includes the following assumptions and
L1531@P48: considerations:
L1532@P48: · Expedited passport fee: Although the historical (fiscal years 2013 through 2020) revenue and obligation data we
L1533@P48: analyzed did not include the expedited passport fee, our modeling assumes full access to these revenues (100 percent
L1534@P48: utilization) given State’s plans to deposit all expedited passport fee revenues into the CBSP account in fiscal year 2022
L1535@P48: and thereafter.
L1536@P48: · Appropriations and special funding: Our model does not assume the provision of additional appropriations to
L1537@P48: support consular services, starting in fiscal year 2022, or the supplemental or annual appropriations Congress provided
L1538@P48: State in fiscal years 2020 and 2021.
L1539@P48: · Individual fees for consular services: Our model reflects retained fee amounts as of November 2021. We assume
L1540@P48: that the fee amounts that State’s Bureau of Consular Affairs (CA) charges for consular services, such as for passports
L1541@P48: and visas, will not change through fiscal year 2026. As such, the passport security surcharge (PSS) increase effective
L1542@P48: December 27, 2021, is not reflected in our modeling.a(For more information, see GAO-22-104424, app. V.)
L1543@P48: · Transfers to the General Fund: We assume the authorities under which CA transfers collections to the General Fund
L1544@P48: remain consistent.bIn addition, we examined the results of our simulations in cases where State’s transfer level
L1545@P48: differed from historical averages. (For more information, see GAO-22-104424, app. V.)
L1546@P48: · Projected costs: We assume projected cost levels reflect both fluctuations in revenues and annual growth by 3
L1547@P48: percent based on the Congressional Budget Office's prediction in the Employment Cost Index. Projected costs are
L1548@P48: based on historical obligations by subaccount and assume that obligations by subaccount remain consistent, other
L1549@P48: than the projected 3 percent annual growth.c Our model does not account for the possibility that State could make
L1550@P48: additional future adjustments in costs.
L1551@P48: · CBSP account carryover balance: To assess State's ability to meet the targeted CBSP account carryover balance of
L1552@P48: 25 percent of obligations, we assume the carryover balance can be lower than zero, though in practice CA's carryover
L1553@P48: balance in future years cannot be lower than zero.
L1554@P48: · Fee-specific subaccount balances: Our model only looks at yearly projected revenue and cost by subaccount, does
L1555@P48: not assume any carryover balances for the subaccounts, and does not account for the possibility that State could make
L1556@P48: additional future adjustments to costs or the subaccount used to cover those costs.
L1557@P48: Source: GAO. | GAO-22-104424

L1560@P48: $316 million in additional annual revenue.

L1867@P57: Projected revenues assume no carryover balances. Projected costs are based on historic obligations
L1868@P57: by subaccount and assume no change other than 3 percent annual growth.
L1869@P57: For more details on the assumptions governing our simulations, see GAO-22-104424, appendix I.

L1889@P58: State officials noted that CA is a service provider that (1) is required to
L1890@P58: transfer some consular fee collections to the General Fund, (2) provides
L1891@P58: some consular services at no cost (“no fee” items for which State cannot
L1892@P58: or has elected not to charge a fee), and (3) is more limited by certain fee
L1893@P58: authorities than others in terms of how it can use some fee revenues.
L1894@P58: These conditions create a structural imbalance in the CBSP account,
L1895@P58: according to State officials, which presents a challenge in continuing to
L1896@P58: fund consular services with fee revenues from a dwindling carryover
L1897@P58: balance. According to State officials, fee revenues with narrow
L1898@P58: expenditure authorities make it difficult for CA to align its obligations fully
L1899@P58: with fee revenues. For example, State retains fees for some visa and
L1900@P58: passport services but does not retain any fees from overseas citizen
L1901@P58: services, which requires it to cover the costs of overseas citizen services
L1902@P58: with fee revenues from visa services, according to State officials.74

L2032@P62: Cost Estimates for Supporting Consular Fees

L2041@P62: We Assessed CA’s Estimation Processes Using Five Key
L2042@P62: Elements of Economic Analysis

L2044@P62: achieve full cost recovery and to project the sufficiency of consular fee
L2045@P62: revenue. We assessed the extent to which these processes meet the five
L2046@P62: key elements of economic analyses82 identified in GAO’s Assessment
L2047@P62: 80State could double-charge for consular fees if, for example, it is permitted to set the
L2048@P62: MRV fee to cover the costs of overseas citizen services for which it already charges a fee,
L2049@P62: but for which it does not retain any fee revenues. Cross-subsidization could occur where
L2050@P62: State charges users for one type of consular services to cover the costs of services
L2051@P62: provided to others. For example, if State is permitted to set the MRV fee (paid as part of
L2052@P62: visa applications by foreign nationals) to cover the costs of overseas citizen services
L2053@P62: (generally provided to U.S. citizens abroad), this could result in cross-subsidization.

L2057@P62: has not considered or not properly dealt with the element.

L2063@P63: Methodology for Economic Analysis: objective and scope, methodology,

L2065@P63: Five Key Elements of Economic Analysis
L2066@P63: 1. Documentation: the documentation included in the analysis

L2068@P63: 3. Analysis of Effects: the analysis of economic effects

L2072@P63: CA Lacks Documentation for Its Analysis of Costs,
L2073@P63: Demand, and Revenue
L2074@P63: CA’s process for estimating cost partly meets the documentation element
L2075@P63: and its processes for estimating demand and revenue do not meet the
L2076@P63: documentation element. According to GAO’s Assessment Methodology,
L2077@P63: the economic analysis should be clearly written with a plain language
L2078@P63: summary. The analysis cites all sources used and documents that it is
L2079@P63: based on the best available economic information. The analysis should
L2080@P63: also document that it complies with a robust quality assurance process
L2081@P63: and, where applicable, the Information Quality Act.84 The analysis
L2082@P63: discloses the use and contributions of contractors and outside
L2083@P63: consultants.85

L2085@P63: estimating costs partly meets the documentation element since it is
L2086@P63: clearly documented, including the method for generating estimates, the
L2087@P63: sources of data, and the use of contractors. CA also documents a robust
L2088@P63: quality assurance process that validates the data prior to their use and by
L2089@P63: 83GAO, Assessment Methodology for Economic Analysis, GAO-18-151SP (Washington,
L2090@P63: D.C.: Apr. 10, 2018).
L2091@P63: 84Pub. L. No. 106-554, § 515, 114 Stat. 2763A-153-4 (Dec. 21, 2000).

L2120@P64: validation compares the results from the current model against prior results. In particular,

L2181@P66: Notes: “Fully meets” means that the economic analysis has considered and properly dealt with the
L2182@P66: element. “Partly meets” means that the economic analysis has only partly considered and properly
L2183@P66: dealt with the element. “Does not meet” means that the economic analysis has either not considered

L2185@P66: Fully meets objective and scope element. We found that CA’s process
L2186@P66: for estimating unit costs fully meets the objective and scope element of
L2187@P66: economic analysis. According to GAO’s Assessment Methodology, an
L2188@P66: economic analysis should explain the action examined and include a
L2189@P66: rationale and justification for the action. The analysis should state its
L2190@P66: objective. The scope of the analysis should be designed to address this
L2191@P66: objective.95 This process fully meets the objective and scope element
L2192@P66: since CA’s estimates of unit cost aim to measure the average global cost
L2193@P66: of providing each consular service, according to CA’s documentation and
L2194@P66: officials. The scope of these estimates is global consular operations. The
L2195@P66: model used in the process, the CoSM, estimates 3 years of costs and the
L2196@P66: estimate of the unit cost is a weighted average of these three estimates,
L2197@P66: therefore taking into account the possibility of future variation in costs.
L2198@P66: Fully meets analysis of effects element. We found that CA’s process
L2199@P66: for estimating unit costs fully meets the analysis of effects element of
L2200@P66: economic analysis. According to GAO’s Assessment Methodology, the
L2201@P66: economic analysis should quantify the important economic effects and
L2202@P66: control for inflation.96 This process fully meets the analysis of effects
L2203@P66: element since it uses data on all direct and indirect costs associated with
L2204@P66:

L2208@P66: Analysis.

L2214@P67: providing consular services and products. CA reported that the CoSM
