L2216@P67: Partly meets methodology element. We found that CA’s process for
L2217@P67: estimating unit costs partly meets the methodology element of economic
L2218@P67: analysis. According to GAO’s Assessment Methodology, the economic
L2219@P67: analysis should consider alternatives that represent all relevant
L2220@P67: alternatives. The analysis should define an appropriate baseline, and
L2221@P67: identify the important economic effects for each alternative considered.97
L2222@P67: CA’s process partly meets the element since it considered an alternative
L2223@P67: estimation approach, according to CA officials. In particular, according to
L2224@P67: CA, it compared the CoSM, which uses expenditures, obligations, and
L2225@P67: allotments, to a model that only considers expenditures and found that
L2226@P67: the alternative model was less accurate.98
L2227@P67: However, CA does not fully meet the element since we cannot assess
L2228@P67: whether the estimates of unit cost achieve CA’s objective of measuring
L2229@P67: the average global costs of providing consular services because of a lack
L2230@P67: of documentation. According to CA officials, the sample of consular posts
L2231@P67: in the CODaC survey used to generate the level of effort data in the
L2232@P67: CoSM are representative of global consular operations. However, CA did
L2233@P67: not provide sufficient documentation to support this assessment. CA
L2234@P67: noted that it will document the process for ensuring that the CODaC
L2235@P67: sample is representative as it further develops its analysis. If the CoSM
L2236@P67: uses data that are not representative of such operations, the resultant unit
L2237@P67: costs may similarly not be representative. More broadly, the use of nonrepresentative unit costs would limit CA’s ability to accurately set prices
L2238@P67: that achieve full cost recovery.
L2239@P67: Partly meets transparency element. We found that CA’s process for
L2240@P67: estimating unit costs partly meets the transparency element of economic
L2241@P67: analysis. According to GAO’s Assessment Methodology, the analysis
L2242@P67: should describe and justify the analytical choices, assumptions, and data
L2243@P67: used. The analysis should assess how plausible adjustments to each
L2244@P67: important analytical choice and assumption affect the estimates of the
L2245@P67: economic effects and the results of the comparison of alternatives. The
L2246@P67: 
L2247@P67: 97Not all parts of the criteria for the methodology element apply to the process of
L2248@P67: estimating unit costs; our discussion focuses on the relevant aspects of the criteria. The
L2249@P67: full criteria for the element are available in GAO’s Assessment Methodology for Economic
L2250@P67: Analysis.
L2251@P67: 98CA’s analysis examined the unit cost for non-petition nonimmigrant visas, which
L2252@P67-68: generate the largest share of revenue for the CBSP, according to CA.Letter
L2253@P68: Page 60 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L2254@P68: analysis should explain the implications of the key limitations in the data
L2255@P68: used. Where feasible, the analysis should adequately quantify how the
L2256@P68: statistical variability of the key data elements underlying the estimates of
L2257@P68: the economic analysis impacts these estimates, and the results of the
L2258@P68: comparison of alternatives.
L2259@P68: CA’s process partly meets the element since it documents its use of
L2260@P68: knowledgeable stakeholders and relevant databases to inform its choice
L2261@P68: of data.99 However, CA does not fully meet the element because it (1)
L2262@P68: does not document its sensitivity tests and (2) does not calculate the
L2263@P68: statistical variability of the level of effort data or the unit costs, which are
L2264@P68: key data elements. CA officials described the sensitivity tests but could
L2265@P68: not provide additional documentation. According to CA, these sensitivity
L2266@P68: tests consider the effect of possible ranges in the estimate for demand
L2267@P68: estimates on cost (and obligations and revenue). CA does not document
L2268@P68: the process for identifying the possible ranges or the process for the
L2269@P68: sensitivity tests. The sensitivity tests do not consider the effect of
L2270@P68: alternate levels of effort. CA explained the choice of sensitivity tests as
L2271@P68: guided by the fact that demand has a greater impact on unit costs than
L2272@P68: level of effort.
L2273@P68: CA’s cost estimates lack transparency because CA does not calculate the
L2274@P68: statistical variability for the level of effort data used in the CoSM or for the
L2275@P68: resultant unit cost estimates.100 CA explained it does not measure the
L2276@P68: statistical variability because it lacks the required software. Consequently,
L2277@P68: CA cannot currently quantify how the variability of the level of effort data
L2278@P68: affects the unit cost estimates.101 However, CA officials stated that they
L2279@P68: are transitioning to modeling software that would allow them to do so.
L2280@P68: Standards for Internal Control in the Federal Government calls for
L2281@P68: 
L2282@P68: 99The actual cost data come from the Comptroller and Global Financial Services and the
L2283@P68: databases that track cost, including GFMS. Prior to the pandemic, the data on estimated
L2284@P68: demand came from the Office of Visa Services, Passport Services, CA, CA Office of the
L2285@P68: Comptroller, and CA Office of the Executive Director, according to CA documentation.
L2286@P68: According to CA officials, workload data come from State databases that track workload.
L2287@P68: Finally, the level of effort data comes from the Passport Agency Task Report, stakeholder
L2288@P68: input, and the CODaC survey.
L2289@P68: 100The level of effort data for international consular affairs come from the CODaC survey,
L2290@P68: which measures a sample of consular posts. Estimates from sample data inherently
L2291@P68: contain statistical variability. Therefore, the resultant cost estimates would also contain
L2292@P68: statistical variability.
L2293@P68: 101CA noted that the statistical uncertainty of other factors, such as demand, might have a
L2294@P68-69: greater impact on unit cost than the statistical uncertainty of level of effort data. Letter
L2295@P69: Page 61 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L2296@P69: management to use quality information to achieve an entity’s objectives,
L2297@P69: such as CA’s objective to achieve full cost recovery.102 Federal internal
L2298@P69: control standards also call for management to analyze and respond to
L2299@P69: risks related to achieving defined objectives. The lack of transparency
L2300@P69: regarding the plausible range of unit costs may limit management’s ability
L2301@P69: to identify, analyze, and respond to risks to full cost recovery arising from
L2302@P69: fluctuations in obligations.
L2303@P69: Conclusions
L2304@P69: Consular fee revenues fully covered the costs of State’s consular
L2305@P69: operations for 7 fiscal years prior to the COVID-19 pandemic, and the use
L2306@P69: of supplemental and annual appropriations and temporary flexibilities in
L2307@P69: fee expenditure authorities helped State address the decline in consular
L2308@P69: fee revenue during the pandemic. However, State continues to face a
L2309@P69: structural imbalance, according to State officials. Specifically, under
L2310@P69: current law, CA is required to transfer some consular fee collections to
L2311@P69: the General Fund while also covering the costs of some services (such as
L2312@P69: overseas citizen services) for which it cannot or does not charge or retain
L2313@P69: consular fee revenue. While the pandemic accelerated and exacerbated
L2314@P69: this imbalance, according to State officials, the imbalance also predated
L2315@P69: the pandemic.
L2316@P69: Additionally, our analysis projects that CBSP account carryover balances
L2317@P69: will decline and are unlikely to meet the targeted threshold if revenues do
L2318@P69: not return to pre-pandemic levels in fiscal year 2022. While it is not known
L2319@P69: when or how quickly demand for consular services will return, State
L2320@P69: officials have indicated that fee revenue may not return to pre-pandemic
L2321@P69: levels for several fiscal years. Even in the event of the most optimistic
L2322@P69: revenue scenario, our analysis shows that the CBSP account carryover
L2323@P69: balance may fall below the targeted threshold by fiscal year 2026.
L2324@P69: A variety of options exist to address projected revenue instability, some of
L2325@P69: which include amending State’s fee authorities, increasing fee amounts,
L2326@P69: appropriating supplemental or annual funding (as has been done during
L2327@P69: the pandemic) and reducing expenditures. State has proposed statutory
L2328@P69: changes each year since 2015 to adjust fee amounts and expand its
L2329@P69: authority to use fee revenues more broadly. However, CA was unable to
L2330@P69: produce any documented analysis or studies of what statutory changes or
L2331@P69: 
L2332@P69-70: 102GAO-14-704G, Principle 13.01. Letter
L2333@P70: Page 62 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L2334@P70: fee adjustments would enable it to reach full cost recovery without
L2335@P70: overcharging, double-charging, or creating a cross-subsidization. State
L2336@P70: was also not able to provide any documented analysis detailing how
L2337@P70: proposed changes to fees it has the authority to set, including the PSS
L2338@P70: fee (which it recently increased by $20) could potentially address the
L2339@P70: decline in fee revenue. As a result, State lacks a comprehensive analysis
L2340@P70: of the various options available to help ensure that revenue and
L2341@P70: expenditures align in the future. Developing a plan for such an analysis
L2342@P70: could help policymakers understand the relative advantages and
L2343@P70: drawbacks of the various options and could inform legislative and policy
L2344@P70: decisions.
L2345@P70: Additionally, CA’s existing processes to estimate costs, revenue, and
L2346@P70: demand do not consider the effect of statistical variability on estimated
L2347@P70: unit costs, and CA does not fully document these processes. This lack of
L2348@P70: documentation prevented us from assessing its demand and revenue
L2349@P70: estimates. Without documentation, management’s ability to identify and
L2350@P70: respond to risks to full cost recovery and its ability to communicate
L2351@P70: organizational information about consular fees to external parties is
L2352@P70: limited.
L2353@P70: Recommendations for Executive Action
L2354@P70: We are making the following three recommendations:
L2355@P70: The Secretary of State should ensure the Bureau of Consular Affairs
L2356@P70: develops a plan to assess and document what fee amounts, statutory
L2357@P70: changes, supplemental and annual funding, or other actions would allow
L2358@P70: State to cover future consular costs. (Recommendation 1)
L2359@P70: The Secretary of State should ensure the Bureau of Consular Affairs
L2360@P70: measures the statistical variability of unit costs to improve the
L2361@P70: transparency of the cost estimates used in the fee-setting process.
L2362@P70: (Recommendation 2)
L2363@P70: The Secretary of State should ensure the Bureau of Consular Affairs fully
L2364@P70: documents its process for generating cost, demand, and revenue
L2365@P70-71: estimates for consular services. (Recommendation 3)Letter
L2366@P71: Page 63 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L2367@P71: Agency Comments and Our Evaluation
L2368@P71: We provided a draft of this report to State for review and comment.
L2369@P71: State’s comments are reprinted in appendix VII. State also provided
L2370@P71: technical comments, which we incorporated as appropriate. State did not
L2371@P71: concur with one of the recommendations, and partially concurred with the
L2372@P71: remaining two recommendations. A summary of agency comments and
L2373@P71: our evaluation follows, and we provide additional specific comments in
L2374@P71: appendix VII.
L2375@P71: State did not concur with our recommendation to develop a plan to
L2376@P71: assess and document what measures would allow State to cover future
L2377@P71: consular costs. According to State’s written comment, State has
L2378@P71: assessed and documented the fee amounts, statutory changes, and
L2379@P71: annual funding necessary to achieve full cost recovery, indicating that its
L2380@P71: cost model presents unrecovered costs by service. We requested
L2381@P71: documentation of such analysis, but State was not able to provide any
L2382@P71: documentation to support that such an analysis had occurred.
L2383@P71: Additionally, State officials have noted that they would calculate the
L2384@P71: effects of changes, including estimates used to set fee amounts, after
L2385@P71: Congress enacts legislation. While performing such analysis can inform
L2386@P71: future State decisions, it still leaves Congress without information that
L2387@P71: would be helpful as it considers State’s requests for legislative changes.
L2388@P71: We maintain that a plan to assess and document which measures would
L2389@P71: be sufficient to cover costs is important because State may be requesting
L2390@P71: statutory changes—for example, authority to set fees or use fee revenues
L2391@P71: more broadly—that do not align with State’s actual needs.
L2392@P71: State partially concurred with our recommendation to measure statistical
L2393@P71: variability of unit costs to improve the transparency of the cost estimates
L2394@P71: used in the fee-setting process. In response, State notes that it calculates
L2395@P71: unit costs based on a deterministic mathematical model. However, State’s
L2396@P71: data on unit cost used in this model still inherently have statistical
L2397@P71: variability (e.g. associated uncertainty) because they come from a survey,
L2398@P71: which introduces statistical variability. We still believe that not considering
L2399@P71: the statistical variability of unit costs may limit management’s ability to
L2400@P71: identify, analyze, and respond to risks to full cost recovery arising from
L2401@P71: fluctuations in costs.
L2402@P71: State partially concurred with our recommendation to fully document its
L2403@P71: process for generating cost, demand, and revenue estimates for consular
L2404@P71-72: services, indicating that they believe they have largely documented their Letter
L2405@P72: Page 64 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L2406@P72: process for estimating costs. As was already included in our draft report,
L2407@P72: State has partially documented its process for generating cost estimates.
L2408@P72: Additionally, as noted in the report, State has not documented its
L2409@P72: processes for demand and revenue estimates. We still believe that
L2410@P72: without fully documenting its processes for cost, demand, and revenue
L2411@P72: estimates, State’s ability to identify and respond to risks to full cost
L2412@P72: recovery is limited.
L2413@P72: We are sending copies of this report to the appropriate congressional
L2414@P72: committees and to the Department of State. In addition, the report is
L2415@P72-73: available at no charge on the GAO website at https://www.gao.gov.Letter

L2425@P74: Page 66 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L2426@P74: Appendix I: Objectives, Scope,
L2427@P74: and Methodology
L2428@P74: This report examines how State manages its consular fee revenue and its
L2429@P74: ability to adapt to the economic challenges of the pandemic, including (1)
L2430@P74: how consular fee revenues compared to obligations prior to the
L2431@P74: pandemic, (2) how State managed the decline in consular fee revenues
L2432@P74: as a result of decreased demand for consular services caused by the
L2433@P74: pandemic, (3) projections regarding State’s Bureau of Consular Affairs’
L2434@P74: (CA) ability to meet the targeted thresholds for the Consular and Border
L2435@P74: Security Programs (CBSP) account in the future, and (4) the extent to
L2436@P74: which State’s processes for estimating key data for evaluating the
L2437@P74: adequacy of its revenue meet the key elements of economic analysis.
L2438@P74: To determine how consular fee revenues and obligations compared prior
L2439@P74: to the pandemic, we analyzed aggregated fee revenue, obligations, and
L2440@P74: carryover balance data provided to us by CA. We obtained this
L2441@P74: information in the form of multiple summary data tables compiled from
L2442@P74: several internal agency databases, including the Global Financial
L2443@P74: Management System (GFMS), State’s official financial system of record.
L2444@P74: We analyzed data from fiscal years 2013 through 2019 to characterize
L2445@P74: CA’s ability to cover the costs of consular services using fee revenue
L2446@P74: during the 7 fiscal years in which consular operations were fully feefunded. We also analyzed relevant State documentation to determine
L2447@P74: which accounts to include in our scope of consular fees. We limited our
L2448@P74: analysis to the CBSP account and the Diplomatic and Consular Programs
L2449@P74: account, which served as the predecessor to the CBSP account, and into
L2450@P74: which most fee revenues were deposited pre-fiscal year 2019.1
L2451@P74: 1According to the President’s Budget for Fiscal Year 2017, in fiscal year 2016 and prior
L2452@P74: years, consular fees were credited in the Diplomatic and Consular Programs account as
L2453@P74: spending authority from offsetting collection. The fiscal year 2017 President’s Budget
L2454@P74: proposed a new stand-alone account, the CBSP account, for State to display fee-funded
L2455@P74: consular programs independent of the larger Diplomatic and Consular Programs account.
L2456@P74: According to the President’s Budget, the change would enable State to make financial
L2457@P74: reporting and budget estimates for these fees and surcharges more easily available to
L2458@P74: users of budget information and other stakeholders. Three of the consular fees for which
L2459@P74: CA retains revenue (the expedited passport fee, the H and L fraud prevention and
L2460@P74: detection fee, and the J-waiver fee) were deposited in accounts other than the CBSP
L2461@P74: account as of December 2021, but we included them in our analysis because they contain
L2462@P74-75: consular fee revenues that are retained by State and used to provide consular services.Appendix I: Objectives, Scope, and
L2463@P75: Methodology
L2464@P75: Page 67 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L2465@P75: To determine how State managed the decline in consular fee revenue as
L2466@P75: a result of decreased demand for consular services caused by the
L2467@P75: pandemic, we analyzed aggregate consular fee revenue, obligations, and
L2468@P75: carryover balance data for fiscal years 2020 through 2021, a period that
L2469@P75: included the onset of the pandemic and the months that followed. We
L2470@P75: analyzed data, primarily from budget documents, such as Congressional
L2471@P75: Budget Justifications, related to supplemental and annual appropriations
L2472@P75: that were provided to State to maintain consular operations impacted by
L2473@P75: coronavirus and offset losses resulting from the coronavirus pandemic,
L2474@P75: among other things. We also reviewed other budget, policy, and planning
L2475@P75: documents related to these annual appropriations. Finally, we interviewed
L2476@P75: knowledgeable State officials to determine how consular fee revenues
L2477@P75: changed during the pandemic and how CA adapted to the challenges
L2478@P75: presented by the pandemic and managed its consular fee revenue
L2479@P75: accordingly.
L2480@P75: To assess the reliability of the supplemental and annual appropriations
L2481@P75: data, we reviewed the data for accuracy and compared the values to
L2482@P75: amounts in official State documents and budget justification documents.
L2483@P75: We determined that these data were sufficiently reliable to document
L2484@P75: State’s receipt and management of these appropriations provided to State
L2485@P75: following the onset of the pandemic.
L2486@P75: To examine projections regarding CA’s ability to meet the targeted
L2487@P75: thresholds for the CBSP account in the future, we developed a Monte
L2488@P75: Carlo simulation model where we performed 10,000 trial runs of simulated
L2489@P75: revenues, using random values of CA’s future revenues.2 This technique
L2490@P75: approximates the likelihood of certain outcomes by performing multiple
L2491@P75: trial runs, called simulations, using random variables within a specified
L2492@P75: range. The simulations capture the volatility of revenues in the projection
L2493@P75: of the future balances of the CBSP account and subaccounts. These
L2494@P75: simulations enabled us to assess, across five different revenue outlook
L2495@P75: scenarios through fiscal year 2026,3 CA’s ability to meet the targeted
L2496@P75: 
L2497@P75: 2We used CA’s 25 percent carryover target by retained fee each year in our model, by
L2498@P75: calculating 25 percent of average historical obligations against the CBSP account as a
L2499@P75: whole. We present this as the benchmark value and use this calculation to determine the
L2500@P75: likelihood of State meeting the targeted threshold.
L2501@P75: 3We decided to report projections through fiscal year 2026 for two main reasons. First,
L2502@P75: longer-term projections inherently contain greater uncertainty. Second, State has
L2503@P75: requested legislative changes regarding its use of CBSP account fees that may affect
L2504@P75-76: future funding prior to fiscal year 2026. Appendix I: Objectives, Scope, and
L2505@P76: Methodology
L2506@P76: Page 68 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L2507@P76: threshold for the CBSP account carryover balance of 25 percent of
L2508@P76: obligations and the likelihood of projected revenue being sufficient to
L2509@P76: cover projected costs that were historically covered by obligations against
L2510@P76: the CBSP account fee-specific subaccounts.4 We based the five revenue
L2511@P76: scenarios on three possible future revenue outlooks, defined as follows:
L2512@P76: · Optimistic, which projects future revenue ranging between an
L2513@P76: absolute deviation above the average of historical revenues and the
L2514@P76: highest revenue in the historical data (fiscal years 2013 through
L2515@P76: 2020). For the CBSP account overall carryover balance, the optimistic
L2516@P76: revenue outlook corresponds to a range of $4.0 billion to $4.1 billion.
L2517@P76: · Neutral, which projects future revenue ranging within one absolute
L2518@P76: deviation below and above the average of historical revenues. For the
L2519@P76: CBSP account overall carryover balance, the neutral revenue outlook
L2520@P76: corresponds to a range of $2.9 billion to $4.0 billion.
L2521@P76: · Pessimistic, which projects future revenue ranging between the lowest
L2522@P76: revenue in the historical data and an absolute deviation below the
L2523@P76: average of historical revenues. For the CBSP account overall
L2524@P76: carryover balance, the pessimistic revenue outlook corresponds to a
L2525@P76: range of $2.3 billion to $2.9 billion.5
L2526@P76: Table 3 summarizes the five scenarios that combine the three possible
L2527@P76: future revenue outlooks.
L2528@P76: 
L2529@P76: 4According to State officials, CA’s official target is to maintain a minimum 25 percent
L2530@P76: carryover by retained fee each year. State officials explained that CA considers 25 percent
L2531@P76: of obligations to be a healthy carryover balance threshold that would ensure CA’s ability to
L2532@P76: make obligations at the beginning of the year. Specifically, State officials mentioned that
L2533@P76: maintaining a healthy carryover balance amount is essential to ensuring CA’s ability to
L2534@P76: make necessary obligations at the beginning of the year, noting that consular fee revenue
L2535@P76: is not received evenly over the course of a year, but instead peaks in spring and summer.
L2536@P76: We have previously found that, for programs where fees are expected to cover all or most
L2537@P76: program costs, and especially when program costs do not necessarily decline with a drop
L2538@P76: in fee collections, a carryover reserve is important.

L2552@P77: Within the model, we also made several assumptions to control for other
L2553@P77: future uncertainties.6
L2554@P77: · Initial carryover balance. We use CA’s fiscal year 2021 carryover
L2555@P77: balance as provided by State officials.7 According to State officials,
L2556@P77: these were preliminary figures as of November 2021 and final data
L2557@P77: will be published as part of the fiscal year 2022 Operating Plan and
L2558@P77: the fiscal year 2023 President’s Budget.
L2559@P77: · Expedited passport fee. Starting in fiscal year 2022, CA will use all
L2560@P77: expedited passport fee revenues for any consular service provided
L2561@P77: through the CBSP account, according to State officials. Although the
L2562@P77: historical data did not include the expedited passport fee, our
L2563@P77: modeling assumes full access to these revenues (100 percent
L2564@P77: utilization), given State’s plans to deposit all expedited passport fee
L2565@P77: revenues in the CBSP account in fiscal year 2022.
L2566@P77: · Appropriations and special funding. Our model does not reflect
L2567@P77: additional appropriations to support consular services, starting in fiscal
L2568@P77: year 2022, or the supplemental or annual funding Congress provided
L2569@P77: State in fiscal years 2020 and 2021.
L2570@P77: · Individual fees for consular services. Our main model reflects
L2571@P77: retained fee amounts as of November 2021. We assume that the fees
L2572@P77: that CA charges for consular services, such as passports and visas,
L2573@P77: will not change through fiscal year 2026. As such, the PSS increase
L2574@P77: effective December 27, 2021 is not reflected in our modeling.
L2575@P77: Assuming the quantity of passport applications demanded remains
L2576@P77: 
L2577@P77: 6The model approximates a simulation based on our set of assumptions and may differ
L2578@P77: with varying conditions.

L2584@P78: similar to historical levels (averaged across fiscal years 2013 through

L2587@P78: · Transfers to the General Fund. We assume the authorities under
L2588@P78: which CA transfers certain fee collections to the General Fund of the
L2589@P78: U.S. Government remain consistent. In addition, we examined how
L2590@P78: the results of our simulations changed if CA’s transfer level differed

L2592@P78: Our model does not reflect the new authority in the Consolidated

L2598@P78: presented for varying transfer rates in appendix V.
L2599@P78: · Projected costs. We assume projected cost levels reflect both
L2600@P78: fluctuations in revenues and annual growth by 3 percent based on the
L2601@P78: Congressional Budget Office’s prediction in the Employment Cost
L2602@P78: Index. Our model does not account for the possibility that CA could
L2603@P78: make additional future adjustments in spending levels. Instead, we
L2604@P78: tested selected alternative cost predictions. We identified an error in
L2605@P78: State’s historical obligations data for fiscal year 2019. According to
L2606@P78: State, the total CBSP obligations for fiscal year 2019 was
L2607@P78: $3,641,307,000, $228,000 less than was reported in the fiscal year
L2608@P78: 2021 Congressional Budget Justification, and a decrease of 0.006
L2609@P78: percent. Our model is based on the total fiscal year 2019 obligations
L2610@P78: published in the Congressional Budget Justification and does not
L2611@P78: account for the revised obligations amount.
L2612@P78: 
L2613@P78: 8We estimated this additional potential revenue by averaging the number of passport
L2614@P78: applications over fiscal years 2013 through 2020 and multiplying that average by the $20
L2615@P78: increase in the PSS fee amount. This calculation assumes that the quantity of passport
L2616@P78: applications demanded will not decrease in response to the fee amount increase and thus
L2617@P78: represents an upper bound of the additional potential revenue.
L2618@P78: 9Pub. L. No. 117-103, § 7069(e) (Mar. 15, 2022).
L2619@P78-79: 10168 Cong. Rec. H3010 (Mar. 9, 2022). Appendix I: Objectives, Scope, and
L2620@P79: Methodology
L2621@P79: Page 71 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L2622@P79: · Carryover balance. In practice, CA’s carryover balance cannot be
L2623@P79: lower than zero.11 To assess CA’s ability to meet the targeted CBSP
L2624@P79: account carryover balance of 25 percent of obligations, we assume
L2625@P79: the carryover balance can be lower than zero.
L2626@P79: On the basis of these assumptions, we calculated the CBSP account
L2627@P79: carryover balance as the sum of the prior year’s carryover balance and
L2628@P79: the current year’s retained collections (i.e., revenue), minus the current
L2629@P79: year’s CA predicted cost, as reflected in the following equation for a
L2630@P79: particular fiscal year t.12
L2631@P79: CBSP account carryover balance (FY t) = CBSP account carryover balance (FY t-1)
L2632@P79: + Revenue (FY t)
L2633@P79:  - Cost (FY t)
L2634@P79: All of our estimated simulations are in nominal dollars. We present the
L2635@P79: median value of the overall CBSP account carryover balance and the
L2636@P79: median value of the difference between projected revenue and cost for its
L2637@P79: fee-specific subaccounts across the 10,000 simulations. We compare the
L2638@P79: median value of the overall CBSP account carryover balance to an
L2639@P79: amount we calculated on the basis of the targeted threshold of having the
L2640@P79: CBSP account carryover balance equal to at least 25 percent of
L2641@P79: obligations. Specifically, over fiscal years 2013 through 2020, obligations
L2642@P79: against the CBSP account averaged $3.21 billion. Using the targeted
L2643@P79: threshold, we calculated the targeted amount to be $802.56 million on the
L2644@P79: basis of the average historical obligations. Appendix V contains further
L2645@P79: results from the simulations, including the expected ranges of the CBSP
L2646@P79: account carryover balance and yearly difference, assuming no carryover
L2647@P79: balances between projected revenue and costs for its fee-specific
L2648@P79: subaccounts.

L2650@P79: carryover balance data from GFMS and other sources used to

L2696@P81: Appendix II: Legal Authorities for
L2697@P81: Selected Consular Fees
L2698@P81: Agencies derive their authority to charge user fees either from the
L2699@P81: Independent Offices Appropriation Act of 1952 (IOAA) or other statutory
L2700@P81: authority.1 The IOAA provides agencies broad authority to assess user
L2701@P81: fees or charges through regulation for services or things of value they
L2702@P81: provide. The IOAA itself does not provide authority for agencies to retain
L2703@P81: fees they collect.2 Some agencies, however, have specific statutory
L2704@P81: authority to retain and use fees without additional legislative action.
L2705@P81: Office of Management and Budget (OMB) Circular No. A-25 establishes
L2706@P81: federal guidelines regarding user fees assessed under the authority of the
L2707@P81: IOAA and other statutes, including the scope and types of activities
L2708@P81: subject to user fees and the basis upon which the fees are set.3 It also
L2709@P81: provides guidance for executive branch agency implementation of fees
L2710@P81: and the disposition of collections.4
L2711@P81: Under OMB Circular No. A-25, agencies must review their user fees for
L2712@P81: agency programs biennially, to include: (1) assurance that existing
L2713@P81: charges are adjusted to reflect unanticipated changes in costs or market
L2714@P81: values, and (2) a review of all other agency programs to determine
L2715@P81: whether fees should be assessed for government services or the user of
L2716@P81: government goods or services.
L2717@P81: The Department of State’s Bureau of Consular Affairs (CA) retains
L2718@P81: revenues from nine consular fees, all of which are charged for visa or
L2719@P81: 
L2720@P81: 1Pub. L. No. 82-137, 65 Stat. 268 (1951), codified at 31 U.S.C. § 9701.
L2721@P81: 2Agencies may have specific statutory authority to deposit fees into receipt accounts but
L2722@P81: may not use them without further congressional appropriation of the funds (offsetting
L2723@P81: receipts) or specific statutory authority to credit the collections to an expenditure account
L2724@P81: and use the fees without additional congressional appropriation (offsetting collections).
L2725@P81: 3OMB, User Charges, Circular No. A-25 (Washington, D.C.: July 8, 1993).
L2726@P81: 4OMB Circular No. A-25 Revised does not apply to the activities of the legislative and
L2727@P81: judicial branches of government or to mixed ownership government corporations as
L2728@P81-82: defined in 31 U.S.C. § 9101.Appendix II: Legal Authorities for Selected

L2835@P84: The Department of State Bureau of Consular Affairs (CA) collects fees for
L2836@P84: some of the consular services it provides. According to State
L2837@P84: documentation, CA transfers some of those fees collected to the General
L2838@P84: Fund of the U.S. Government (General Fund) (including all fees collected
L2839@P84: in providing overseas citizen services) but retains collections (revenues)
L2840@P84: from nine consular fees: the (1) machine readable visa fee, (2) passport
L2841@P84: security surcharge, (3) Western Hemisphere Travel Initiative surcharge,

L2843@P84: diversity visa lottery fee, (7) H and L fraud prevention and detection fee,
L2844@P84: (8) affidavit of support fee, and (9) J-waiver fee.1

L2847@P84: and Foreign Service.2

L3832@P102: Simulation Results for the Consular and
L3833@P102: Border Security Programs Account
L3834@P102: Page 94 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L3835@P102: Appendix V: Further Details and
L3836@P102: Additional Simulation Results for
L3837@P102: the Consular and Border Security
L3838@P102: Programs Account
L3839@P102: This appendix provides further details and additional simulation results for
L3840@P102: our model of the overall Consular and Border Security Programs (CBSP)
L3841@P102: account carryover balance and the difference between projected revenue
L3842@P102: retained each year and projected costs for its fee-specific subaccounts.
L3843@P102: For each of these we provide the ranges we used in constructing the
L3844@P102: revenue outlooks as well as tables to present the range of values,
L3845@P102: corresponding to the 10th and 90th percentiles from across the
L3846@P102: simulations, along with the 50th percentile, corresponding to the median
L3847@P102: value.1 We also present results showing the median value across
L3848@P102: additional simulations we conducted for the CBSP account carryover
L3849@P102: balance with varying rates of transfers (12 percent, 8 percent, and 4
L3850@P102: percent) to the General Fund of the U.S. Government (General Fund).2
L3851@P102: The Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2022, provides a new authority that
L3852@P102: requires that, for fiscal year 2022 and thereafter, passport application and
L3853@P102: execution fees be deposited in the CBSP account.3 According to the act’s
L3854@P102: joint explanatory statement, this shifts passport application and execution
L3855@P102: fees currently deposited in the General Fund to State.4 While our model
L3856@P102: does not reflect this change, we identify a potential rate of transfer that
L3857@P102: may approximate the effect of the estimated revenue. In addition, we
L3858@P102: present potential results showing the median value for the CBSP account
L3859@P102: carryover balance incorporating the $20 increase in the passport security
L3860@P102: surcharge (PSS) fee amount, effective December 27, 2021.
L3861@P102: 
L3862@P102: 1The median, or 50th percentile, refers to the middle value when the values are arranged
L3863@P102: in order of size.
L3864@P102: 2State is required by law to deposit certain consular fee collections into the General Fund.
L3865@P102: In this report, we refer to consular fees collected but not retained by State as “transfers to
L3866@P102: the General Fund.”
L3867@P102: 3Pub. L. No. 117-103, § 7069(e) (Mar. 15, 2022).
L3868@P102-103: 4168 Cong. Rec. H3010 (Mar. 9, 2022).Appendix V: Further Details and Additional

L3915@P104: Notes: We rely on historical data from State’s Bureau of Consular Affairs from the period of fiscal
L3916@P104: years 2013 through 2020. The lower bound and upper bound refer to the lowest and highest value in
L3917@P104: each of the revenue outlooks, whose construction varies. Specifically, the optimistic revenue outlook
L3918@P104: corresponds to future revenues falling between one absolute deviation above the historical average
L3919@P104: and the highest revenue in the historical data. Neutral revenue outlook corresponds to future
L3920@P104: revenues falling within one absolute deviation below and above the historical average. Pessimistic
L3921@P104: revenue outlook corresponds to future revenues falling between the lowest revenue in the historical
L3922@P104: data and one absolute deviation below the average of historical revenues. Absolute deviation is the
L3923@P104: average of the absolute value of the difference between the data points and their mean.
L3924@P104: “All other subaccounts” includes the immigrant visa security surcharge, diversity visa lottery fee, H
L3925@P104: and L fraud prevention and detection fee, affidavit of support fee, and J-waiver fee subaccounts.
L3926@P104: While the H and L fraud prevention and detection fee and the J-waiver fee are used narrowly to cover
L3927@P104: certain consular expenses, they are deposited into accounts other than the Consular and Border
L3928@P104: Security Programs account. We constructed the revenue outlook ranges for all other subaccounts
L3929@P104-105: based on the sum of all the fees included in all other subaccounts.Appendix V: Further Details and Additional

L4400@P118: Additional Simulation Results Based on Varying Rates of
L4401@P118: Transfers to the General Fund
L4402@P118: We also present results showing the median value and ranges across
L4403@P118: additional simulations we conducted for the CBSP account carryover
L4404@P118: balance with varying rates of transfers to the General Fund. Over fiscal
L4405@P118: years 2013 through 2019, CBSP has transferred on average 16 percent
L4406@P118: of its collections to the General Fund. We conducted simulations under
L4407@P118: the hypothetical situations where CBSP would transfer a smaller
L4408@P118: percentage of its collections, specifically 12 percent, 8 percent, and 4
L4409@P118: percent.6 Figure 15 shows the median of the projected CBSP account
L4410@P118: carryover balance across our simulations with the expedited passport fee
L4411@P118: revenue from fiscal years 2022 through 2026 assuming that transfers to
L4412@P118: the General Fund are 12 percent of collections.7
L4413@P118: 
L4414@P118: 6In fiscal years 2020, 2021, and 2022, there were changes to the fee amounts for the
L4415@P118: minor border crossing card, domestic authentications, and the Passport Security
L4416@P118: Surcharge, respectively. These recent fees are only partially reflected in the historical
L4417@P118: collection data on which our model is based. As a result, our model does not fully account
L4418@P118: for these changes in future collections. Additionally, our model does not reflect the new
L4419@P118: authority signed into law on March 15, 2022 as part of the Consolidated Appropriations
L4420@P118: Act, 2022, which provides that, beginning October 1, 2021, and for each fiscal year
L4421@P118: thereafter, passport application and execution fees shall be deposited in the CBSP
L4422@P118: account. Pub. L. No. 117-103, § 7069(e). According to the act’s joint explanatory
L4423@P118: statement, this authority shifts passport application and execution fees currently deposited
L4424@P118: in the General Fund to State to support consular operations. 168 Cong. Rec. H3010 (Mar.
L4425@P118: 9, 2022). Historically, State transferred 16 percent of its collections on average annually to
L4426@P118: the General Fund. Of these transfers, passport application fees accounted for roughly
L4427@P118: three-quarters of transfers to the General Fund on average annually, with other fees
L4428@P118: accounting for approximately one-quarter of the transfers. Given this new authority to
L4429@P118: retain the passport application fee, State may instead transfer approximately 4 percent on
L4430@P118: average annually to the General Fund, a quarter of what it used to transfer on annual
L4431@P118: average. For more information, see our simulation for hypothetical situations where 4
L4432@P118: percent of collections are transferred to the General Fund for what could be an
L4433@P118: approximation of this potential scenario.
L4434@P118: 7The carryover balance we present includes the balance from fees that State historically
L4435@P118: used broadly (such as the MRV fee, which State used to cover the cost of providing
L4436@P118: consular services generally), as well as those fees that State used for narrower purposes
L4437@P118: (such as the PSS, which State used only to cover the cost of enhanced border securityrelated consular services) because of differences in their expenditure authorities. Our
L4438@P118: simulation results present the total carryover balance, without consideration of the
L4439@P118: purposes for which those amounts may be used. Recent acts of Congress provided State
L4440@P118: with temporary expenditure flexibilities for some of these narrower fee authorities during
L4441@P118: the pandemic. These temporary flexibilities in State’s consular fee expenditure authorities
L4442@P118: will end, however, causing these authorities to return to their prior statutory language and
L4443@P118: expenditure authority, unless additional temporary or permanent statutory changes are
L4444@P118-119: made.Appendix V: Further Details and Additional

L4461@P119: retained by State as “transfers to the General Fund.” We rely on historical data from State’s Bureau of
L4462@P119: Consular Affairs covering the period of fiscal years 2013 through 2020. We consider 25 percent of the
L4463@P119: average historical obligations because according to State officials, CA’s official target is to maintain a
L4464@P119: minimum 25 percent carryover balance by retained fee each year. Over fiscal years 2013 through
L4465@P119: 2020, obligations against the CBSP account averaged $3.21 billion. We used CA’s target, calculating
L4466@P119: 25 percent of average historical obligations against the CBSP account as a whole. We present this as

L4634@P124: 9The carryover balance we present includes the balance from fees that State historically
L4635@P124: used broadly (such as the MRV fee, which State used to cover the cost of providing
L4636@P124: consular services generally), as well as those fees that State used for narrower purposes
L4637@P124: (such as the PSS, which State used only to cover the cost of enhanced border securityrelated consular services) because of differences in their expenditure authorities. Our
L4638@P124: simulation results present the total carryover balance, without consideration of the
L4639@P124: purposes for which those amounts may be used. Recent acts of Congress provided State
L4640@P124: with temporary expenditure flexibilities for some of these narrower fee authorities during
L4641@P124: the pandemic. These temporary flexibilities in State’s consular fee expenditure authorities
L4642@P124: will end, however, causing these authorities to return to their prior statutory language and
L4643@P124: expenditure authority, unless additional temporary or permanent statutory changes are
L4644@P124: made.
L4645@P124: 10Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2022, Pub. L. No. 117-103, § 7069(e). This new
L4646@P124: authority provides that, beginning October 1, 2021, and for each fiscal year thereafter,
L4647@P124: passport application and execution fees shall be deposited in the CBSP account.
L4648@P124: According to the act’s joint explanatory statement, this shifts passport application and
L4649@P124: execution fees currently deposited in the General Fund of the Treasury to the Department
L4650@P124: of State to support consular operations. 168 Cong. Rec. H3010 (Mar. 9, 2022).
L4651@P124: Historically, State transferred 16 percent of its collections on average annually to the
L4652@P124: General Fund. Of these transfers, passport application fees accounted for roughly threequarters of transfers to the General Fund on average annually, with other fees accounting
L4653@P124: for approximately one-quarter of the transfers. Given this new authority to retain the
L4654@P124: passport application fee, State may instead transfer approximately 4 percent on average
L4655@P124-125: annually to the General Fund, a quarter of what it used to transfer on annual average.Appendix V: Further Details and Additional

L4738@P127: Additional Potential Simulation Results Incorporating
L4739@P127: Increase in PSS Fee
L4740@P127: The PSS fee amount increase effective December 27, 2021, changed the
L4741@P127: fee amount by $20, from $60 to $80. We estimated that, assuming the
L4742@P127: quantity of passport applications demanded remains similar to historical
L4743@P127: levels (averaged across fiscal years 2013 through 2020), the $20
L4744@P127: increase in the PSS fee amount could result in up to $316 million in
L4745@P127: additional annual revenue. Specifically, we estimated this additional
L4746@P127: potential revenue by multiplying 15,777,597, the average number of
L4747@P127: passport applications over fiscal years 2013 through 2020, by the $20
L4748@P127: increase in the PSS fee amount. This calculation assumes that the
L4749@P127: quantity of passport applications demanded will not decrease in response
L4750@P127: to the fee amount increase and thus represents an upper bound of the
L4751@P127-128: additional potential revenue. Figure 18 shows the median of the projected Appendix V: Further Details and Additional

L4799@P130: Changes to Consular Fee Authorities

L4801@P130: Appendix VI: Department of State
L4802@P130: Requests for Changes to
L4803@P130: Consular Fee Authorities
L4804@P130: The Department of State is permitted by a combination of statutes and
L4805@P130: internal agency decisions to set, charge, retain, and use revenues from
L4806@P130: some consular fees. However, State is required by law to transfer some
L4807@P130: consular fees to the General Fund of the U.S. Government (General
L4808@P130: Fund).1 Additionally, some statutes are more narrow than others in how
L4809@P130: State can use revenues from some consular fees, according to State
L4810@P130: officials.
L4811@P130: State’s Bureau of Consular Affairs (CA) shifted to covering the costs of
L4812@P130: consular operations with consular fee revenues starting in fiscal year
L4813@P130: 2013.2 From fiscal years 2013 through 2019, consular operations were
L4814@P130: fully fee-funded. State is required to transfer some consular fee
L4815@P130: collections to the General Fund and transferred an annual average of 16
L4816@P130: percent of consular fee collections from fiscal years 2013 through 2019.
L4817@P130: Additionally, a growing share of consular fee carryover balances3 were in
L4818@P130: fee-specific subaccounts associated with narrower fee authorities. The
L4819@P130: requirement to transfer some fee collections to the General Fund and
L4820@P130: narrower authority on how State can use some revenues have created a
L4821@P130: structural imbalance in the account in which CA manages consular fee
L4822@P130: revenues and obligations, according to State officials.
L4823@P130: According to State officials, to address this structural imbalance, State
L4824@P130: has requested changes to established consular fee authorities from
L4825@P130: 
L4826@P130: 1State is required by law to deposit certain consular fee collections into the General Fund.
L4827@P130: In this report, we refer to the consular fees collected but not retained by State as “transfers
L4828@P130: to the General Fund.”
L4829@P130: 2State’s nine retained consular fees were established by different statutes and at different
L4830@P130: times, although all were established before CA transitioned to fully funding consular
L4831@P130: operations with fee revenue in fiscal year 2013.
L4832@P130: 3We refer to unobligated funding from State’s retained fees in the CBSP account and
L4833@P130: other accounts remaining across fiscal years as “carryover balances.” State refers to
L4834@P130-131: carryover balances as “carryforward balances.”Appendix VI: Department of State Requests for
L4835@P131: Changes to Consular Fee Authorities
L4836@P131: Page 123 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L4837@P131: Congress, and these have been included in every State Congressional
L4838@P131: Budget Justification from fiscal years 2015 through 2022.
L4839@P131: The evolution of State’s requests to Congress as it relates to increased
L4840@P131: flexibility for using consular fee revenue has reflected both State’s needs
L4841@P131: and the revenue fluctuations it has experienced. State requested changes
L4842@P131: to consular fee authorities several years prior to the onset of the COVID19 pandemic.4 State’s requested changes have ranged from asking for
L4843@P131: authority to use certain fee revenues more broadly and extending
L4844@P131: pandemic-era temporary expenditure flexibilities, to requesting to set fees
L4845@P131: to cover the costs of other consular services where State charges no fee
L4846@P131: or does not retain a fee.
L4847@P131: State requested several permanent changes to broaden its consular feesetting authorities in its fiscal year 2022 Congressional Budget
L4848@P131: Justification. For more information on State’s requests for permanent
L4849@P131: amendments to its statutory consular fee authorities, see table 26.

L4855@P131: State; (2) expand the expenditure authority for the H and L fraud prevention and detection

L5052@P139-140: cc: GAO – Jason Bair CA – Rena Bitter OIG - Norman BrownAppendix VII: Comments from the Department of State
L5053@P140: Page 132 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L5054@P140: Page 2
L5055@P140: Department of State of Response to GAO Draft Report
L5056@P140: CONSULAR AFFAIRS: State May Be Unable to Cover Projected Costs if
L5057@P140: Revenues Do Not Quickly Rebound to Pre-Pandemic Levels
L5058@P140: (GAO-22-104424, GAO Code 104424)
L5059@P140: Thank you for the opportunity to comment on the GAO draft report, entitled “Consular
L5060@P140: Affairs: State May Be Unable to Cover Projected Costs if Revenues Do Not Quickly
L5061@P140: Rebound to Pre-Pandemic Levels.”
L5062@P140: Recommendation 1: The Secretary of State should ensure the Bureau of Consular
L5063@P140: Affairs develops a plan to assess and document what fee amounts, statutory
L5064@P140: changes, supplemental and annual funding, or other actions would allow State to
L5065@P140: cover future consular costs.
L5066@P140: Response: The Department respectfully does not concur with this recommendation.
L5067@P140: The Department has assessed and documented the fee amounts, statutory changes,
L5068@P140: annual funding, and other amounts that would be necessary to achieve full cost
L5069@P140: recovery since the FY 2016 cost model, which fully disclosed unrecovered costs by
L5070@P140: service. This information was provided to GAO along with information demonstrating
L5071@P140: which fee authorities are problematic due to spending limitations. As GAO
L5072@P140: recognizes, except for the supplemental appropriations received during the COVID19 pandemic, CA has been “fully” fee funded since FY 2013. Notwithstanding that,
L5073@P140: the Department has remitted approximately 16 percent of consular fee collections to
L5074@P140: Treasury. As long as there is a structural imbalance between CA’s fee-retention
L5075@P140: authorities and the costs of the services it provides, the Department will not be able
L5076@P140: to achieve full cost recovery.
L5077@P140: The Department emphasizes that consular fees are set by calculating unit costs
L5078@P140: assuming a certain level of demand. The Department calculates the costs of all
L5079@P140: consular fees, including non-retained fee services and no-fee services, annually. The
L5080@P140: Department does not have the statutory authority to incorporate into the amount of
L5081@P140: the fees it retains the cost of other services for which fee-retention authority does not
L5082@P140: exist. The inability to retain a fee does not impact the unit cost of that fee or any
L5083@P140: other; the unit cost and demand assumptions are the same whether the Department
L5084@P140: retains the fee or remits it to Treasury. Therefore, the ability to retain additional fees
L5085@P140: would not result in CA collecting fees more than its costs. For example, Congress
L5086@P140: recently passed legislation authorizing full fee retention of the passport application
L5087@P140-141: fee; historically $28 of this fee has been remitted to Treasury. Once implemented, Appendix VII: Comments from the Department of State
L5088@P141: Page 133 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L5089@P141: this change will alter neither the unit cost nor the demand assumptions for the fee.
L5090@P141: The only impact will be that CA will now be able to recover the full cost of this
L5091@P141: service, reducing the shortfall in its revenue by $28 multiplied by the same demand
L5092@P141: assumption. If a legislative change occurred that included an additional charge that is
L5093@P141: not currently part of the cost model, then a model update would be required to recalculate fees based on this new legislation. In the event of any such legislation in
L5094@P141: the future, CA would be sure to execute the necessary updates to the model.
L5095@P141: The Department requests that GAO modify the summary section of the draft report,
L5096@P141: specifically on the second page of “What GAO Found,” and later in the report the
L5097@P141: conclusions
L5098@P141: Page 3
L5099@P141: at pages 38-42, to remedy the issues noted with this recommendation. Additionally,
L5100@P141: the Department notes the structural imbalance issues documented throughout the
L5101@P141: report (page 1 of the report, Appendix 2, and pages 19-22, 27-28, 36-42, and the
L5102@P141: second sentence in the conclusions section). The Department suggests edits to the
L5103@P141: second paragraph of “What GAO Found” as follows:
L5104@P141: “The Consular and Border Security Programs (CBSP) has what State officials
L5105@P141: refer to as a ‘structural imbalance’ under which it does not charge fees for all
L5106@P141: services provided, does not retain the fees it collects for certain services, and
L5107@P141: does not have broad expenditure authorities for many of its fee accounts. This
L5108@P141: structural imbalance requires CA to use retained fee revenue, such as from
L5109@P141: visa services, to cover unfunded services, such as U.S. citizen services,
L5110@P141: despite an inability to factor the costs of those services into the fees for which
L5111@P141: revenue is retained or otherwise set retained fees above cost. State has
L5112@P141: requested statutory changes to its authorities to set, retain, and expend
L5113@P141: consular fees so that it can fully cover costs and/or have more flexibility in
L5114@P141: how it can use revenue. GAO does not believe CA has sufficiently documented
L5115@P141: the potential impact the requested changes would have. CA, however, has
L5116@P141: conducted analysis and modeling of legislative proposals to identify the ones
L5117@P141: most likely to support the Consular and Border Security Programs and State
L5118@P141: officials noted that total unrecovered costs and associated fee estimates
L5119@P141: would be recalculated after any legislation is enacted.”
L5120@P141: The Department also suggests edits to the second full paragraph on page 49 as
L5121@P141-142: follows:Appendix VII: Comments from the Department of State
L5122@P142: Page 134 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L5123@P142: “A variety of options exist to address projected revenue instability, some of
L5124@P142: which
L5125@P142: include expanding State’s legal authorities, increasing fees, appropriating
L5126@P142: funding (as has been done during the pandemic) and reducing expenditures.
L5127@P142: State has proposed statutory changes each year since 2015 to broaden its
L5128@P142: expenditure authorities and to remedy the structural imbalance identified in
L5129@P142: this report, which requires CA to use retained fee revenue to cover unfunded
L5130@P142: services, despite an inability to factor the costs of those services into the fees
L5131@P142: for which revenue is retained or otherwise set retained fees above cost. CA
L5132@P142: has conducted analysis and modeling of legislative proposals to identify the
L5133@P142: ones most likely to support the Consular and Border Security Program and
L5134@P142: State officials noted that total unrecovered costs and associated fee estimates
L5135@P142: would be recalculated after any legislation was enacted. Any such analysis
L5136@P142: must be comprehensive, and CA should identify all of the various options
L5137@P142: available to help ensure that revenue and expenditures align in the future. A
L5138@P142: clearer analysis could help policymakers understand the relative advantages
L5139@P142: and drawbacks of the various options and could inform legislative and policy
L5140@P142: decisions.”
L5141@P142: The Department additionally suggests corresponding changes throughout the report
L5142@P142: to similar language.
L5143@P142: · Recommendation 2: The Secretary of State should ensure the Bureau of
L5144@P142: Consular Affairs measures the statistical variability of unit costs to improve the
L5145@P142-143: transparency of the cost estimates used in the fee setting process.Appendix VII: Comments from the Department of State
L5146@P143: Page 135 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L5147@P143: Page 4
L5148@P143: Response: The Department partially concurs with this recommendation.
L5149@P143: The Department utilizes activity-based costing recommended within OMB Circular A25 to ensure fees are set to achieve full cost recovery to the U.S. government. This
L5150@P143: means that unit costs are calculated based on a deterministic mathematical model,
L5151@P143: not a statistical model aimed at predicting future behaviors. The Department concurs
L5152@P143: that a sensitivity analysis should be conducted and has performed this analysis on
L5153@P143: unit costs. The results of that analysis indicate that demand fluctuations have nearly
L5154@P143: seven times the impact on the unit costs than the level of effort in terms of interview
L5155@P143: and adjudication time.
L5156@P143: · Recommendation 3: The Secretary of State should ensure the Bureau of
L5157@P143: Consular Affairs fully documents its process for generating cost, demand, and
L5158@P143: revenue estimates for consular services.
L5159@P143: Response: The Department partially concurs with this recommendation.
L5160@P143: This recommendation is already largely fulfilled because, as the GAO report
L5161@P143: acknowledges, the Department’s process for estimating costs has been clearly
L5162@P143: documented, including the methodology, sources of data, and use of contractors.
L5163@P143: Throughout the course of this engagement, the Department provided nearly 100
L5164@P143: documents in response to over 200 questions and held 20 briefings and meetings.
L5165@P143: Included in the materials provided to GAO was documentation transparently outlining
L5166@P143: the process for generating costs and the actual cost data used to calculate fees and
L5167@P143: future cost assumptions. In addition, the Department supplied extensive details on
L5168@P143: the consular cost drivers, shared the activity dictionary, and provided papers on the
L5169@P143: generation of demand estimates. The Department also provided the results of a
L5170@P143: sensitivity analysis that showed how demand fluctuations have a greater impact than
L5171@P143: variations in the level of effort on the unit costs of non-petition based nonimmigrant
L5172@P143: visas. The responses provided full transparency on the financial impact that nonretained fees have on consular operations, the cost incurred to provide those
L5173@P143: services, and the average percentage of consular fees remitted to the Treasury.
L5174@P143: The Department does believe, however, that documentation could be improved by
L5175@P143: organizing the processes for estimating demand and revenue into one
L5176@P143: comprehensive document for a broader audience, and has already begun this
L5177@P143-144: process.Appendix VII: Comments from the Department of State
L5178@P144: Page 136 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L5179@P144: GAO Comments
L5180@P144: 1. Unrecovered costs by service and observations on which fee authorities have
L5181@P144: narrower spending authority are examples of the types of information that could
L5182@P144: be useful to decision-makers in weighing options that could allow State to cover
L5183@P144: future consular costs. Given the variety of options that exist to address projected
L5184@P144: revenue instability (such as amending State’s fee and related legal authorities,
L5185@P144: increasing fees, appropriating funding, and reducing expenditures), we believe a
L5186@P144: plan to assess and document which actions in isolation or in combination would
L5187@P144: be sufficient is important to reach full cost recovery without overcharging, doublecharging, or creating a cross-subsidization.
L5188@P144: 2. State noted the ability to retain additional fees would not result in CA collecting
L5189@P144: fees in excess of its costs. This assertion has not been true in the past. As noted
L5190@P144: in the report, State retained total annual fee revenues in excess of total annual
L5191@P144: costs each year from fiscal years 2013 through 2019, resulting in a carryover
L5192@P144: balance of $2.0 billion in fiscal year 2019. State accumulated this carryover
L5193@P144: balance despite transferring an annual average of 16 percent of collected fees to
L5194@P144: the General Fund.1
L5195@P144: 3. State asserts that “the only impact will be that CA will now be able to recover the
L5196@P144: full cost of this service.” State’s ability to retain the passport application fee under
L5197@P144: the new fee retention authority could have several potential ramifications, such
L5198@P144: as
L5199@P144: · The newly retained passport application fee revenue may free up resources
L5200@P144: to cover the costs of other services. For example, State may choose to use
L5201@P144: some of the newly retained passport application fee revenue instead of
L5202@P144: revenue from the machine readable visa fee to cover the cost of overseas
L5203@P144: citizen services.
L5204@P144: · State retaining the passport application and execution fees will result in an
L5205@P144: estimated minimum of $340,000,000 in additional resources for consular
L5206@P144: operations in fiscal year 2022, according to the joint explanatory statement
L5207@P144: accompanying the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2022.2 However, in turn,
L5208@P144: this will result in that estimated $340,000,000 not being transferred to the
L5209@P144: General Fund, thus reducing the balance of the General Fund.
L5210@P144: · State could reassess its requests for legislative changes to its fee authorities.
L5211@P144: State has consistently requested a variety of legislative changes to help it
L5212@P144: 1The General Fund of the U.S. Government is managed by the Department of the Treasury. State is
L5213@P144: required by law to deposit certain consular fee collections into the General Fund, which we refer to as
L5214@P144: “transfers to the General Fund.”
L5215@P144-145: 2168 Cong. Rec. H3010 (Mar. 9, 2022).Appendix VII: Comments from the Department of State
L5216@P145: Page 137 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L5217@P145: achieve full cost recovery. The authority to retain hundreds of millions of
L5218@P145: dollars in revenue could affect State’s analysis of which of these changes are
L5219@P145: still necessary and whether they are a priority.
L5220@P145: 4. As reflected in our report and in State’s comments, State officials have noted that
L5221@P145: they would calculate the effects of changes, including estimates used to set fee
L5222@P145: amounts, after Congress enacts legislation. While performing such analysis can
L5223@P145: be helpful in informing future State decisions, it still leaves Congress without
L5224@P145: information that could be helpful as it considers State’s requests for legislative
L5225@P145: changes. We continue to believe that such analysis should be performed,
L5226@P145: documented, and shared with policymakers to enable them to understand the
L5227@P145: potential impact of each option, in isolation or in combination, before taking
L5228@P145: legislative action.
L5229@P145: 5. State asserts that it “has conducted analysis and modeling of legislative
L5230@P145: proposals to identify the ones most likely to support the Consular and Border
L5231@P145: Security Programs.” We requested documentation of such analysis in October
L5232@P145: 2021, but the documentation that State provided was insufficient and did not
L5233@P145: support that such an analysis had occurred. Without a documented cohesive
L5234@P145: plan of what fee authorities are required to achieve full cost recovery, State may
L5235@P145: be requesting statutory changes—for example, authority to set fees or use fee
L5236@P145: revenues more broadly—that do not align with State’s actual needs.
L5237@P145: 6. State’s model for calculating unit costs uses estimates of the level of effort—i.e.
L5238@P145: the amount of time it takes a State official to complete a consular service such as
L5239@P145: adjudicating a visa application. Since these estimates are based on a survey of a
L5240@P145: sample of employees, they inherently have statistical variability (e.g. associated
L5241@P145: uncertainty). Consequently, the resulting estimates of unit costs also have
L5242@P145: statistical variability despite the use of a deterministic mathematical model. As we
L5243@P145: highlight in our report, a lack of knowledge of the statistical variability of unit costs
L5244@P145: may limit management’s ability to identify, analyze, and respond to risks to full
L5245@P145: cost recovery arising from fluctuations in obligations.
L5246@P145: 7. As our reports reflects, we believe this recommendation is not already largely
L5247@P145: fulfilled—State has only partially documented one process and has not
L5248@P145: documented the other two. Specifically, as noted in our report, according to State
L5249@P145: officials, the processes for demand and revenue estimates are not currently
L5250@P145: documented. Further, State has partially documented its process for generating
L5251@P145: cost estimates, leaving key parts of its process undocumented.
L5252@P145: 8. During the 20 months of our review (August 2020 to April 2022), State officials
L5253@P145: met with us 13 times. Of these 13 meetings, two were GAO briefings to State on
L5254@P145: our model for projecting future carryover balances and our results, and two were
L5255@P145: the standard entrance and exit conferences. These meetings, and the totality of
L5256@P145-146: the questions asked and documents requested, related to the entire scope of our Appendix VII: Comments from the Department of State
L5257@P146: Page 138 GAO-22-104424 Consular Affairs
L5258@P146: review, not just our analysis of State’s processes for generating cost, demand,
L5259@P146-147: and revenue estimates.Appendix VIII: GAO Contact and Staff Acknowledgments

L5270@P147-148: (104424)GAO’s Mission
L5271@P148: The Government Accountability Office, the audit, evaluation, and investigative arm
L5272@P148: of Congress, exists to support Congress in meeting its constitutional
L5273@P148: responsibilities and to help improve the performance and accountability of the
L5274@P148: federal government for the American people. GAO examines the use of public
L5275@P148: funds; evaluates federal programs and policies; and provides analyses,
L5276@P148: recommendations, and other assistance to help Congress make informed
L5277@P148: oversight, policy, and funding decisions. GAO’s commitment to good government
L5278@P148: is reflected in its core values of accountability, integrity, and reliability.
